**1. "observe" → "demonstrate"**
三架构 6/6 Blindspot + 7 统计方法 + 44/44 NLI sweep 已达 "demonstrate" 门槛。在以下位置升级：
- Abstract: "we observe" → "we demonstrate" (for empirical findings)
- Conclusion: similar upgrade
- 但保留 agnostic stance 不变——解释层面仍用 "whether... the operational consequence is identical"

**2. Llama verbosity GEE Orig OR 精确措辞**
Llama verbosity Orig OR=0.378 (p=0.020) 是三 judge 中唯一 Orig OR 显著的。目前 "style-dominated" 表述对 Llama 需要限定。建议：
- 如果当前写的是 "all three judges show non-significant originality effects for verbosity"，需要修正
- 改为类似 "verbosity is predominantly style-driven across all three judges (Style OR 8–13 vs. Orig OR 0.38–0.49), though Llama-70B shows a small but significant negative originality effect (OR=0.38, p=.020)"
- "style-dominated" 的核心结论不变（Style OR 仍远大于 |Orig OR|），但需诚实标注 Llama 的差异

**3. Debiased Prompt 三架构升级**

"Debiased prompting shows consistent partial efficacy across three independent architectures (Δ = −0.4pp to −13.1pp), with all five judge×axis combinations remaining significant after debiasing (all p < 0.001). This confirms that style bias is deeply embedded and cannot be fully addressed through prompt engineering alone."

### Tool result

_source line 3370 · 2026-05-21T17:05:35.588834+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM judges are widely deployed for evaluation and alignment, yet style features alone explain judge scores with near-perfect correlation.
3	We introduce Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), the first framework combining NLI-gated content control, per-axis style decomposition, and position-controlled pairwise comparison to systematically quantify style bias in LLM judges.
4	Across three target judges (GPT-4o, Qwen3-32B, Llama-3.3-70B) and one negative control (Claude Sonnet 4), three benchmarks, and three style axes, CSD reveals the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects (BT 63--84\%, $p < 0.001$), following a severity spectrum---complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$), exceeding 60\% attenuation at stronger effects---confirmed by seven statistical methods.
5	GEE conditional decomposition reveals a per-axis confound structure consistent across all three target judges: verbosity bias is style-dominated (Style OR $8$--$13$, Originality OR ${<}1$), formality exhibits a conditional effect moderated by content quality changes, and register is originality-dominated---determining which estimates are directly interpretable and which require conditional analysis.
6	A fourth judge (Claude Sonnet 4) detects no bias on either axis, supporting discriminative validity.
7	We identify two candidate interpretations---measurement attenuation and construct divergence---and show that both yield the same operational conclusion: Likert-only evaluation systematically misses style-driven preferences that propagate through pairwise-based alignment pipelines.
8	\end{abstract}
9

### Tool result

_source line 3372 · 2026-05-21T17:05:36.091386+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a black-box framework yielding two complementary findings across four judges (three target, one negative control), three benchmarks, and three style axes.
5	First, the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects, following a severity spectrum---complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$), over 60\% attenuation at stronger effects---confirmed by seven statistical methods.
6	Whether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, the operational consequence is identical: Likert-only audits miss style-driven preferences that propagate through pairwise-based alignment pipelines.
7	
8	Second, GEE conditional decomposition reveals a per-axis confound structure consistent across three independent architectures---verbosity is style-dominated, formality conditional, register originality-dominated---determining which estimates are directly interpretable and which require conditional analysis, and directly informing debiasing priorities.
9	
10	For the evaluation community: style bias audits should adopt content-controlled pairwise comparison with per-axis decomposition, especially at the moderate effect sizes typical of real-world evaluation ($n < 500$), where six experimental controls bound the risk of format-level amplification (\S\ref{sec:amplification}; \citealt{jeong2025comparative}).
11	Existing Likert-based null findings may reflect measurement insensitivity rather than genuine absence of bias.
12	Future work should incorporate human evaluation of rewriting quality, broader benchmark and axis coverage, and stronger causal identification through randomized rather than observational designs.
13

140	\caption{Detection probability at observed effect magnitudes. 80\% power crossings: sign test $n \approx 62$, BT $n \approx 114$, binarized Likert $n \approx 462$, Likert ATE $n \approx 1{,}473$. At typical scales ($n = 50$--$200$, shaded), pairwise methods reach 80\% power while Likert methods remain far below. At $n = 355$, Likert $d = {-}0.025$ confirms the gap is structural, not merely a power issue.}
141	\label{fig:power_curve}
142	\end{figure}
143	
144	\subsection{Cross-Judge Replication}
145	\label{sec:cross_judge}
146	
147	Two independent judges replicate the Blindspot: Qwen3-32B (BT 66.5\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.908$) and Llama-3.3-70B (BT 69.5\%, $p = 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.846$), spanning three architectures (proprietary API, open 32B, open 70B).
148	Qwen3-32B's higher GEE estimate may partly reflect evaluator-producer bias (Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).
149	
150	\subsection{Cross-Axis Replication: Verbosity}
151	\label{sec:cross_axis}
152	
153	The verbosity axis provides the strongest and cleanest demonstration of the Blindspot.
154	All three target judges show consistent verbosity preference: GPT-4o 76.0\% ($p = 0.0001$), Qwen3-32B 77.3\% ($p < 0.0001$), and Llama-70B 73.5\% ($p = 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant for all three ($p = 0.403$, $0.179$, $0.752$).
155	Unlike formality, verbosity is style-dominated across all three judges: GEE decomposition confirms massive style effects (OR $= 8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$) while originality ORs are below 1 ($0.38$--$0.49$), indicating any artifact favors rewrites rather than originals (Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
156	Both rewriting directions favor verbose responses (concise$\to$verbose 90.7\%, verbose$\to$concise 80.6\%); controlling for length leaves the style OR unchanged ($<$2\%; all $p > 0.27$).
157	
158	\subsection{Register: Weak Evidence with Strong Originality Confound}
159	\label{sec:negative_control}
160

_source line 3376 · 2026-05-21T17:06:05.423478+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	LLM judges are central infrastructure for NLP evaluation (AlpacaEval, MT-Bench, Chatbot Arena) and alignment training (RLHF, DPO), where judge-generated preferences serve as reward signals.
4	Any systematic bias in their assessments propagates through both evaluation leaderboards and model development pipelines.
5	
6	Recent work suggests that style features alone explain most of the variance in judge scores \citep{murugadoss2025sos}, motivating systematic measurement of per-axis style bias.
7	\citet{li2026preference} demonstrate that 76--92\% of observed preference signals originate from surface-level style cues rather than substantive quality differences, and \citet{soumik2026judging} report bias scores of 0.76--0.92 across five different judges.
8	Despite this consensus on severity, existing approaches each address individual requirements: CASSE \citep{casse2026concise} and FairJudge \citep{wang2026fairjudge} provide multi-dimensional style analysis, RATE \citep{reber2025rate} introduces interventional design, and length-controlled AlpacaEval \citep{dubois2025lc} offers population-level correction.
9	However, no single framework integrates all four requirements---per-axis decomposition, interventional identification, per-instance diagnostics, and black-box applicability.
10	A further concern compounds these limitations: standard Likert scoring suffers from a measurement efficiency gap---integer discretization produces ${\sim}60\%$ uninformative ties, and even non-tied pairs carry weaker signal than pairwise comparison---a limitation predicted by measurement theory \citep{thurstone1927law} but previously unquantified for LLM judges.
11	
12	We propose Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), the first framework combining NLI-gated content control, per-axis style decomposition, and position-controlled pairwise comparison to systematically quantify style bias in LLM judges.
13	Inspired by interventional approaches to text analysis \citep{vei

```json
  "old_string": "Debiased prompting partially reduces but never eliminates bias across all three judges: verbosity drops $6.6$--$13.1$pp (GPT-4o $76.0\\% \\to 62.9\\%$; Llama-70B $73.5\\% \\to 66.9\\%$), formality drops $0.4$--$5.7$pp (Llama-70B $69.5\\% \\to 64.3\\%$; Qwen3-32B $-5.7$pp; GPT-4o $-0.4$pp), with all debiased estimates remaining significant.\nThis model-independent resistance to debiasing indicates style preferences are embedded in judge representations, not prompt-level artifacts.",
  "new_string": "Debiased prompting shows consistent partial efficacy across three independent architectures ($\\Delta = {-}0.4$pp to ${-}13.1$pp): verbosity drops $6.6$--$13.1$pp (GPT-4o $76.0\\% \\to 62.9\\%$; Llama-70B $73.5\\% \\to 66.9\\%$), formality drops $0.4$--$5.7$pp (Llama-70B $69.5\\% \\to 64.3\\%$; Qwen3-32B $-5.7$pp; GPT-4o $-0.4$pp), with all five debiased judge$\\times$axis estimates remaining significant (all $p < 0.001$).\nThis confirms that style bias is deeply embedded in judge representations and cannot be fully addressed through prompt engineering alone."
}
```

84	\subsection{Measurement Sensitivity: Likert vs.\ Pairwise}
85	\label{sec:likert_pairwise}
86	
87	\paragraph{Head-to-head comparison with RATE.}
88	Applying RATE's Likert ATE methodology \citep{reber2025rate} to our formality style-transformed pairs yields Cohen's $d = 0.10$, $p = 0.33$ (non-significant, $n = 88$); pairwise comparison on identical data reveals a 63.0\% formal preference ($p = 0.0004$, Bradley-Terry on all 173 comparisons), confirmed by a consistency-filtered sign test (73.9\%, $p = 0.006$, $n = 46$ consistent pairs).
89	This is the Likert Blindspot: the same data, the same style intervention, two measurement instruments---one detects bias, the other does not.
90	A $4\times$ scaled replication ($n = 355$) distinguishes measurement attenuation from insufficient power: the Likert effect size shrinks to $d = {-}0.025$ (near zero) while BT remains stable at $67.5\%$ ($p < 0.0001$)---if the gap were merely statistical power, $d$ would remain non-zero at larger $n$.
91	Bootstrap subsampling across seven sample sizes confirms this pattern: $d$ remains flat near zero ($d_{\text{median}} = {-}0.017$) as CIs narrow from ${\pm}0.3$ to ${\pm}0.1$, never excluding zero (Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_attenuation}).
92	Attenuation follows a severity spectrum.
93	At moderate bias (BT 63--67\%), it is complete: $d$ converges to ${\approx}0$ even at $n = 355$, rendering the bias invisible to any Likert-based test.
94	At stronger effects (BT ${>}70\%$), Likert partially detects but severely attenuates: MT-Bench formality yields Likert $d \approx 0.15$ for BT ${\sim}70\%$ effects---${>}60\%$ signal compression---explaining why three of eleven bias-positive cells escape the Blindspot (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, $\ddagger$ rows).
95	
96	The two frameworks are complementary: RATE measures aggregate quality effects via Likert ATE; CSD targets per-axis style preference via forced-choice comparison, which eliminates ties and is more sensitive to ordinal preferences.
97	The

24	\section{GEE Model Diagnostics}
25	\label{app:gee_diagnostics}
26	
27	\paragraph{Correlation structure robustness.}
28	Table~\ref{tab:gee_corr} compares exchangeable and independence working correlation structures across all six judge$\times$axis cells.
29	The two structures yield virtually identical results: $|\Delta\text{QIC}| < 0.001$ and maximum coefficient change ${<}0.02$ in every cell, with estimated within-pair correlations $\hat{\alpha} \in [-0.25, +0.11]$.
30	All GEE results reported in the main text are therefore robust to correlation structure specification.
31

52	To verify that the additive GEE specification is appropriate, we test a style$\times$originality interaction term on formality data for both judges.
53	The interaction is non-significant in both cases (GPT-4o: $p = 0.53$; Qwen3-32B: $p = 0.32$), confirming that the additive model is the correct specification and that style and originality effects operate independently.

```json
    "old_string": "Figure~\\ref{fig:power_curve} plots detection probability curves at observed effect magnitudes (not post-hoc power; cf.\\ \\citealt{hoenig2001abuse}): Likert requires ${\\approx}4.5\\times$ more data to detect the same bias.",
    "new_string": "Figure~\\ref{fig:power_curve} plots detection probability curves at observed effect magnitudes (not post-hoc power; cf.\\ \\citealt{hoenig2001abuse}): Likert requires ${\\approx}4.5\\times$ more data to detect the same bias.\nFormal power analysis across all six judge$\\times$axis cells confirms: Likert would require $4$--$206\\times$ the current sample size ($336$--$27{,}159$ pairs) to detect effects that BT identifies at $p < 0.001$ with $n \\leq 100$; for formality, the required samples exceed $18{,}000$ pairs---orders of magnitude beyond typical evaluation budgets (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis})."
```

```json
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Correlation structure robustness.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:gee_corr} compares exchangeable and independence working correlation structures across all six judge$\\times$axis cells.\nThe two structures yield virtually identical results: $|\\Delta\\text{QIC}| < 0.001$ and maximum coefficient change ${<}0.02$ in every cell, with estimated within-pair correlations $\\hat{\\alpha} \\in [-0.25, +0.11]$.\nAll GEE results reported in the main text are therefore robust to correlation structure specification.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{GEE correlation structure comparison across six judge$\\times$axis cells.}\n\\label{tab:gee_corr}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & Exch.\\ QIC & Indep.\\ QIC & $|\\Delta\\text{QIC}|$ & Max$|\\Delta\\text{Coef}|$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-4o form.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nQwen3 form.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nGPT-4o verb.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nQwen3 verb.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nGPT-4o reg.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nQwen3 reg.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interaction test.}\nTo verify that the additive GEE specification is appropriate, we test a style$\\times$originality interaction term on formality data for both judges.\nThe interaction is non-significant in both cases (GPT-4o: $p = 0.53$; Qwen3-32B: $p = 0.32$), confirming that the additive model is the correct specification and that style and originality effects operate independently.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Correlation structure robustness.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:gee_corr} compares exchangeable and independence working correlation structures across all eight judge$\\times$axis cells.\nThe two structures yield virtually identical results: $|\\Delta\\text{QIC}| < 0.001$ and maximum coefficient change ${<}0.02$ in every cell, with estimated within-pair correlations $\\hat{\\alpha} \\in [-0.25, +0.11]$.\nAll GEE results reported in the main text are therefore robust to correlation structure specification.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{GEE correlation structure comparison across eight judge$\\times$axis cells.}\n\\label{tab:gee_corr}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & Exch.\\ QIC & Indep.\\ QIC & $|\\Delta\\text{QIC}|$ & Max$|\\Delta\\text{Coef}|$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-4o form.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nQwen3 form.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nLlama-70B form.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nGPT-4o verb.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nQwen3 verb.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nLlama-70B verb.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nGPT-4o reg.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\nQwen3 reg.\\ & --- & --- & ${<}0.001$ & ${<}0.02$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interaction test.}\nAdding a Style$\\times$Originality interaction term to the additive GEE specification yields no significant interactions in any of the eight judge$\\times$axis cells (all $p > 0.13$, range $0.14$--$0.96$), confirming that the additive model is the correct specification and that regime classifications are robust to model specification."
}
```

```json
}
```

38	\section*{Limitations}
39	\label{sec:limitations}
40	
41	\paragraph{Quality--style confounding and construct validity.}
42	The NLI gate blocks content changes but cannot block quality perception shifts mediated by style itself---a fundamental identifiability limit of text-rewriting approaches.
43	Although the formality effect survives logistic adjustment for original preference ($\text{OR} = 2.32$, $p = 0.006$; Appendix~\ref{app:direction}), residual confounding is not fully eliminated.
44	An alternative interpretation of the Blindspot is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs: Likert captures absolute quality difference while pairwise captures ordinal preference. Under this view, the divergence reflects construct difference rather than measurement failure. The Comparative Trap \citep{jeong2025comparative} lends support to this alternative; six experimental controls bound this risk (\S\ref{sec:amplification}), though definitive separation would require human ground-truth ratings.
45

We discuss quality--style confounding and construct validity, evaluator-producer bias and generalizability limits, and validation methodology constraints.\n\\item Did you discuss any potential risks of your work?\n\\textbf{Yes.} Our framework detects existing biases in deployed LLM judges; it does not introduce new risks. We discuss the risk that the Comparative Trap (\\S\\ref{sec:amplification}) may amplify measured preferences beyond true effects, and bound this with six controls.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{B. Ethical Considerations.}\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item Does this paper use or create datasets that include personally identifiable information or offensive content?\n\\textbf{No.} All benchmarks (LLMBar, AlpacaEval, MT-Bench) are publicly available and contain instruction-response pairs without PII.\n\\item Did you discuss whether and how consent was obtained from people whose data you are using?\n\\textbf{N/A.} We use only publicly released benchmarks with established community norms for research use.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{C. Reproducibility.}\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item Are all details about the experimental setup presented or referenced?\n\\textbf{Yes.} Section~\\ref{sec:method} describes the pipeline; Appendix~\\ref{app:implementation} provides API versions, hyperparameters, NLI model specification, and random seeds. 0	\section{Experiments}
1	\label{sec:experiments}

3	
4	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
5	\label{sec:stat_framework}
6	
7	We designate a primary family of eight hypothesis tests (three judges $\times$ formality and verbosity, plus register for GPT-4o and Qwen3-32B) and control the family-wise error rate with Holm-Bonferroni correction at $\alpha = 0.05$.
8	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports both raw and adjusted $p$-values.
9	Analyses outside this grid are classified as either \emph{exploratory} (NLI threshold sensitivity sweep, \S\ref{sec:nli_threshold}) or \emph{confirmatory} (consistency filter validation, \S\ref{sec:consistency}).
10	Exploratory analyses are reported with uncorrected $p$-values and interpreted as hypothesis-generating; confirmatory analyses test specific hypotheses about pipeline validity and are evaluated at the nominal $\alpha = 0.05$ level without additional correction, as they address orthogonal questions about measurement reliability rather than the primary style-bias family.
11	Bradley-Terry parameters are estimated via spectral initialization followed by minorization-maximization \citep{hunter2004mm}, using the default regularization of the \texttt{choix} Python library.
12	All bootstrap confidence intervals use 10{,}000 pair-level resamples with seed 42.
13

155	All three target judges show consistent verbosity preference: GPT-4o 76.0\% ($p = 0.0001$), Qwen3-32B 77.3\% ($p < 0.0001$), and Llama-70B 73.5\% ($p = 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant for all three ($p = 0.403$, $0.179$, $0.752$).
156	Unlike formality, verbosity is style-dominated across all three judges: GEE decomposition confirms massive style effects (OR $= 8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$) while originality ORs are below 1 ($0.38$--$0.49$)---though Llama-70B's is the only significant one (OR $= 0.38$, $p = .020$)---indicating any artifact favors rewrites over originals rather than reflecting quality confounding (Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
157	Both rewriting directions favor verbose responses (concise$\to$verbose 90.7\%, verbose$\to$concise 80.6\%); controlling for length leaves the style OR unchanged ($<$2\%; all $p > 0.27$).
158	
159	\subsection{Register: Weak Evidence with Strong Originality Confound}
160	\label{sec:negative_control}
161	
162	Register provides weak but directionally consistent evidence (GPT-4o BT 57.0\%, $p = 0.04$; Qwen3-32B 55.3\%, $p = 0.076$; Likert n.s.).
163	GEE decomposition reveals the strongest originality confound ($\text{OR} = 5.72$, $p < 0.0001$) alongside a marginally significant style effect ($\text{OR} = 1.91$, $p = 0.046$; Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
164	This exploratory finding does not enter the primary Holm-Bonferroni family.
165

167	\label{sec:direction}
168

225	\section{Bootstrap Attenuation Analysis}
226	\label{app:bootstrap_attenuation}
227	
228	To distinguish measurement attenuation from insufficient statistical power, we subsample the $n = 355$ formality replication (Qwen3-32B) at seven sample sizes and compute Likert Cohen's $d$ with bootstrap 95\% CIs at each (Table~\ref{tab:attenuation}).
229	Under a power limitation, $d$ would remain constant while only $p$-values improve with larger $n$; under measurement attenuation, $d$ itself converges to zero.

_source line 3482 · 2026-05-21T17:18:59.444600+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a black-box framework yielding two complementary findings across four judges (three target, one negative control), three benchmarks, and three style axes.
5	First, the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects, following a severity spectrum---complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$), over 60\% attenuation at stronger effects---confirmed by seven statistical methods.
6	Whether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, the operational consequence is identical: Likert-only audits miss style-driven preferences that propagate through pairwise-based alignment pipelines.
7	
8	Second, GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates a per-axis confound structure consistent across three independent architectures---verbosity is style-dominated, formality conditional, register judge-dependent---determining which estimates are directly interpretable and which require conditional analysis, and directly informing debiasing priorities.
9	
10	For the evaluation community: style bias audits should adopt content-controlled pairwise comparison with per-axis decomposition, especially at the moderate effect sizes typical of real-world evaluation ($n < 500$), where six experimental controls bound the risk of format-level amplification (\S\ref{sec:amplification}; \citealt{jeong2025comparative}).
11	Existing Likert-based null findings may reflect measurement insensitivity rather than genuine absence of bias.
12	Future work should incorporate human evaluation of rewriting quality, broader benchmark and axis coverage, and stronger causal identification through randomized rather than observational designs.
13

160	\subsection{Register: Judge-Dependent Confound Structure}
161	\label{sec:negative_control}
162	
163	Register shows a judge-dependent pattern: Llama-70B exhibits the strongest effect (BT 63.5\%, $p = 0.0014$; Likert n.s.), GPT-4o shows a weaker signal (BT 57.0\%, $p = 0.04$), and Qwen3-32B is non-significant (BT 55.3\%, $p = 0.076$).
164	GEE decomposition reveals that the register confound structure varies across judges: Qwen3-32B is originality-dominated (Orig OR $= 5.72$, $p < 0.0001$; Style OR $= 1.91$, $p = 0.046$), while GPT-4o and Llama-70B show mixed regimes with comparable style and originality effects (Llama-70B: Style OR $= 3.33$, $p = 0.0003$; Orig OR $= 3.07$, $p = 0.0008$; Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
165	These exploratory findings do not enter the primary Holm-Bonferroni family.
166	
167	\subsection{GEE Conditional Decomposition and Confound Structure}
168	\label{sec:direction}
169	
170	GEE conditional decomposition \citep{liang1986gee} with exchangeable working correlation and robust standard errors separates style from originality effects (Appendix~\ref{app:direction}; model diagnostics including correlation structure robustness and interaction tests in Appendix~\ref{app:gee_diagnostics}).
171	After controlling for original preference, the formality effect remains significant (GPT-4o: OR $= 2.58$, $p = 0.002$; Qwen3-32B: OR $= 2.15$, $p < 0.0001$, $n = 355$; Llama-70B: OR $= 4.81$, $p < 0.0001$; Table~\ref{tab:gee}); the significant originality ORs confirm residual confounding.
172	The $4\times$ replication ($n = 355$) confirms the Blindspot: BT $67.5\%$ (halved CI), Likert n.s.\ ($p = 0.60$).
173

175	\centering
176	\small
177	\caption{GEE conditional decomposition (exchangeable correlation) of style and originality effects. Verbosity is style-dominated across all three judges; formality and register show comparable style effects with varying originality confounds.}

```json
  "old_string": "Same-style double-rewrite controls confirm a rewriting artifact: judges prefer LLM-polished text over originals (BT $P_{\\text{orig}}$: formality $.42/.38$; verbosity $.17/.08$; all $p < .04$; Appendix~\\ref{app:artifact_control}), validating GEE decomposition: the artifact cancels in GEE's originality term, leaving Style ORs uncontaminated.\nDebiased prompting shows consistent partial efficacy across three independent architectures ($\\Delta = {-}0.4$pp to ${-}13.1$pp): verbosity drops $6.6$--$13.1$pp (GPT-4o $76.0\\% \\to 62.9\\%$; Llama-70B $73.5\\% \\to 66.9\\%$), formality drops $0.4$--$5.7$pp (Llama-70B $69.5\\% \\to 64.3\\%$; Qwen3-32B $-5.7$pp; GPT-4o $-0.4$pp), with all five debiased judge$\\times$axis estimates remaining significant (all $p < 0.001$).\nThis confirms that style bias is deeply embedded in judge representations and cannot be fully addressed through prompt engineering alone.",
  "new_string": "Same-style double-rewrite controls confirm a rewriting artifact (BT $P_{\\text{orig}}$: $.08$--$.42$; Appendix~\\ref{app:artifact_control}), absorbed by GEE's originality term.\nDebiased prompting partially reduces but never eliminates bias across three architectures ($\\Delta = {-}0.4$ to ${-}13.1$pp), with all five debiased estimates remaining significant (all $p < 0.001$), confirming that style preferences are embedded in judge representations."
```

[Director 指令] [Patch — Round 6 综合修复] 上次派的 patches 因 session 重置未执行，需要全部重新做。按优先级排序：

**MF1 (CRITICAL — desk reject 风险): 主文必须 ≤8 页**
当前 Conclusion (§6) 溢出约 8-10 行到 page 9。修复方案：
1. 压缩 §5.5 Register 段（当前约 7 行描述三个 judge 的 GEE register 结果）→ 精简到 3 行，重点只保留 "register 呈 judge-dependent 异质性" 结论
2. 压缩 §6 Conclusion 本身——去掉重复的证据枚举，保留核心 takeaway
3. 目标：主文 page 8 底部结束，page 9 从 Limitations 开始

**SF1: 限定 "first" novelty claim**
- `sections/related_work.tex` line 23: "first systematic quantification of this gap for LLM judge style bias" → 改为 "first systematic quantification of this gap for LLM judge style bias via controlled per-axis style decomposition"
- 如 Abstract/Intro 有类似 "first" claim，同样限定到具体方法组合

**SF3: 因果术语统一**
- `sections/introduction.tex` line 20: "A black-box interventional framework" → "A black-box controlled framework"
- 仅改这一处。§3.5 的 hedging 已经合适，不需要改。

**SF4: Generalizability boundary**
- §6 Conclusion 末尾加一句："Our findings are established on English benchmarks with n ≤ 355; validation on larger corpora, additional languages, and emerging judge architectures remains necessary."

**SF5: TOST Δ=15pp 外部 justification**
- 在 TOST 相关段落（§5.3 或 Appendix）加引用：AlpacaEval length-controlled bias 的效应量约 15-20pp 作为外部参照。如找不到精确引用，至少加一句 "We set Δ = 15 percentage points, corresponding to the smallest treatment effect observed across our three target judges, providing a conservative equivalence bound."

**SF6: Assumption (iii) axis-specific confounds**
- 在 Limitations 段补充 1-2 句：同款式重写控制可检测通用 rewriting artifact，但无法检测 axis-specific confounds（如 formality 改写可能同时引入 vocabulary sophistication 变化）。

**MF3: Evaluator-producer cross-ref**
- §5.4 首次提到 Qwen3-32B 结果时加一句 cross-ref："Qwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge; cross-rewriter validation (Appendix D) confirms the Blindspot pattern holds with an independent producer (GPT-4o rewriter: BT 64.4%, Likert p = .838), though Style OR decreases 22% (13.10 → 10.17), bounding the producer effect."
- Limitations 中 evaluator-producer 段加 register caveat："Register lacks cross-rewriter validation entirely; its bias estimates should be interpreted with this caveat."

**Minor 修复（如页面空间允许）：**
- M1: Abstract 精简——移除 OR 值细节，保留 BT 百分比范围
- M5: Table 1 第 6 列加 header（如有第 6 列缺 header 的情况）
- M7: bib key wu2023style 年份检查——如果 venue 年份与 key 年份不一致，修正 bib entry
- R6-REF1: 在 Related Work 加 Shi et al. (2024) "Judging the Judges" 和 Bavaresco et al. (2024) "LLMs Instead of Human Judges" 引用（需先在 references.bib 添加 bib entry）

**优先级**：MF1 页面溢出是 desk-reject 风险，必须第一个修。修完 MF1 后编译验证主文 ≤8 页再继续其他 patches。

1	\section{Experiments}

5	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
6	\label{sec:stat_framework}
7	
8	We designate a primary family of eight hypothesis tests (three judges $\times$ formality and verbosity, plus register for GPT-4o and Qwen3-32B) and control the family-wise error rate with Holm-Bonferroni correction at $\alpha = 0.05$.
9	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports both raw and adjusted $p$-values.
10	Analyses outside this grid are classified as either \emph{exploratory} (NLI threshold sensitivity sweep, \S\ref{sec:nli_threshold}) or \emph{confirmatory} (consistency filter validation, \S\ref{sec:consistency}).
11	Exploratory analyses are reported with uncorrected $p$-values and interpreted as hypothesis-generating; confirmatory analyses test specific hypotheses about pipeline validity and are evaluated at the nominal $\alpha = 0.05$ level without additional correction, as they address orthogonal questions about measurement reliability rather than the primary style-bias family.
12	Bradley-Terry parameters are estimated via spectral initialization followed by minorization-maximization \citep{hunter2004mm}, using the default regularization of the \texttt{choix} Python library.
13	All bootstrap confidence intervals use 10{,}000 pair-level resamples with seed 42.

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	Two lines of work motivate our approach: interventional methods for diagnosing judge bias, and measurement theory on the sensitivity of different elicitation formats.
5	
6	\paragraph{Interventional Approaches to Judge Bias}
7	Text-based interventional methods have a growing methodological foundation \citep{feder2021causalm,wu2021polyjuice,feder2022causal}.
8	\citet{reber2025rate} propose RATE, a framework that rewrites model outputs along style attributes using imperfect counterfactuals and measures the causal effect on reward model scores via Likert-scale Average Treatment Effect (ATE).
9	RATE applies a correction term to account for rewriting imperfections, but its reliance on Likert ATE as the primary metric is a vulnerability: our experiments show that Likert scoring exhibits a ceiling effect in which 60\% of style-transformed pairs receive identical integer scores, yielding non-significant ATE estimates ($p = 0.332$) for the same interventions that pairwise comparison detects at $p = 0.006$.
10	If this Likert Blindspot generalizes beyond our setting, RATE's own style-related findings may underestimate the true magnitude of attribute effects.
11	CSD differs from RATE in three respects: it targets individual style axes rather than general attributes, it measures bias through position-controlled pairwise comparison rather than Likert ATE, and it enforces content preservation via NLI entailment gating rather than post-hoc correction for imperfect rewrites.
12	\citet{counterfactual2025rhetorical} also adopt an interventional LLM-based design, rewriting texts to measure rhetorical style effects and aggregating preferences with a Bradley-Terry model.
13	Their framework operates at persona-level granularity (e.g., ``writes like a professor''), whereas CSD intervenes on specific style axes (e.g., formality, conciseness) and uses a sign test with position control rather than parametric aggregation.
14	
15	\paragrap

38	\section*{Limitations}
39	\label{sec:limitations}
40	
41	\paragraph{Quality--style confounding and construct validity.}
42	The NLI gate blocks content changes but cannot block quality perception shifts mediated by style itself---a fundamental identifiability limit of text-rewriting approaches.
43	Although the formality effect survives logistic adjustment for original preference ($\text{OR} = 2.32$, $p = 0.006$; Appendix~\ref{app:direction}), residual confounding is not fully eliminated.
44	An alternative interpretation of the Blindspot is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs: Likert captures absolute quality difference while pairwise captures ordinal preference. Under this view, the divergence reflects construct difference rather than measurement failure. The Comparative Trap \citep{jeong2025comparative} lends support to this alternative; six experimental controls bound this risk (\S\ref{sec:amplification}), though definitive separation would require human ground-truth ratings.
45

1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Direction Asymmetry and Quality Confounding}
4	\label{app:direction}
5	
6	The formality axis exhibits a pronounced direction asymmetry: the judge selects the formal variant in 78.8\% of formal-is-rewrite pairs ($p = 0.001$) but only 54.5\% of casual-is-rewrite pairs ($p = 0.83$).
7	This asymmetry suggests that original quality $Q$ and formality $S$ are partially confounded---formally written texts may be inherently perceived as higher quality.
8	To quantify this, we fit a GEE logistic model (exchangeable correlation) with both formality and original-preference as predictors.
9	After controlling for original preference, the formality effect remains significant ($\text{OR} = 2.58$, $p = 0.002$), and the original-preference effect is also significant ($\text{OR} = 2.32$, $p = 0.006$).
10	The formality preference thus survives adjustment for original-preference confounding, though the significant original-preference effect indicates that quality--style confounding is present and non-negligible.
11	
12	At $n = 88$, the Qwen3-32B formality Style OR ($4.20$) exceeded the GPT-4o estimate ($2.58$), but a scaled replication ($n = 355$) corrects this to $2.15$ ($p < 0.0001$), comparable to GPT-4o and consistent with small-sample upward bias rather than evaluator-producer inflation of the style effect.
13	The originality confound remains stronger ($4.99$ vs.\ $2.32$), consistent with evaluator-producer bias inflating the originality component (Limitations).
14	The directional agreement between judges---both favor formal text after controlling for originality---supports the existence of formality bias, but the effect magnitude from Qwen3-32B should not be directly compared with GPT-4o.
15	
16	The NLI gate blocks \emph{content} changes but cannot block quality perception shifts that are themselves mediated by style---a fundamental identifiability limit of any text-rewriting approach.
17

```json
{
```
```json
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_5/projects/causal_style_decomp_judge/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex",
```
```json
  "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a black-box framework yielding two findings across four judges (three target, one negative control), three benchmarks, and three style axes.\nFirst, the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects---complete at moderate bias ($d \\to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---confirmed by seven statistical methods.\nWhether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, Likert-only audits miss style-driven preferences that propagate through pairwise-based alignment pipelines.\nSecond, GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates per-axis confound structure consistent across three architectures---verbosity style-dominated, formality conditional, register judge-dependent---determining which estimates require conditional analysis and informing debiasing priorities.\n\nStyle bias audits should adopt content-controlled pairwise comparison with per-axis decomposition, especially at moderate effect sizes ($n < 500$), where six experimental controls bound amplification risk (\\S\\ref{sec:amplification}; \\citealt{jeong2025comparative}); existing Likert-based null findings may reflect measurement insensitivity rather than genuine absence of bias.\nOur findings are established on English benchmarks with $n \\leq 355$; validation on larger corpora, additional languages, and emerging judge architectures remains necessary, as does human evaluation of rewriting quality and stronger causal identification through randomized designs.\n"
}
```

</details>

```json
  "old_string": "Formal equivalence testing \\citep[TOST;][]{lakens2017equivalence} at $\\Delta = 10$pp is underpowered at $n = 87$; at $\\Delta = 15$pp (corresponding to a small-to-medium effect in evaluation contexts) both axes confirm equivalence, bounding any Claude style preference to ${<}15$pp---substantively distinct from GPT-4o ($13$--$23$pp) and Qwen3-32B ($17$--$27$pp).",
  "new_string": "Formal equivalence testing \\citep[TOST;][]{lakens2017equivalence} at $\\Delta = 10$pp is underpowered at $n = 87$; at $\\Delta = 15$pp both axes confirm equivalence, bounding any Claude style preference to ${<}15$pp---substantively distinct from GPT-4o ($13$--$23$pp) and Qwen3-32B ($17$--$27$pp).\nThe $\\Delta = 15$pp margin represents a conservative upper bound: it exceeds all observed negative-control deviations from 50\\% (Claude: $+6.2$/$+4.1$pp) while remaining below the smallest confirmed bias ($+13$pp for GPT-4o formality), ensuring the equivalence zone excludes effects we would classify as genuine bias."
}
```

Based on the search results, here's what I found about the Shi et al 2024 work on LLM judge bias evaluation:

## Overview

The paper "Judging the Judges: A Systematic Study of Position Bias in LLM-as-a-Judge" examines how LLMs tend to favor solutions based on their position within the prompt, compromising reliability as evaluators.

## Key Findings

The findings confirm that position bias is not due to random chance and varies significantly across judges and tasks. While position bias is weakly influenced by the length of prompt components, it is strongly affected by the quality gap between solutions.

Judges may systematically favor responses based on order (primacy/recency preference), with the magnitude modulated by model family, context window, and candidate quality gap.

Based on the search results, here is the information about the paper arxiv 2406.07791 "Judging the Judges":

**Title:** Judging the Judges: A Systematic Study of Position Bias in LLM-as-a-Judge

**Authors:** Lin Shi, Chiyu Ma, Wenhua Liang, Xingjian Diao, Weicheng Ma, Soroush Vosoughi

**Submission Details:** Submitted on 12 Jun 2024 (v1), last revised 11 Nov 2025 (this version, v9)

**Overview:** The paper addresses how LLM-as-a-Judge has emerged as a promising alternative to human evaluators, yet inherent biases - particularly position bias, the tendency to favor solutions based on their position within the prompt - compromise its reliability.

_source line 3592 · 2026-05-21T18:14:04.806579+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a black-box framework yielding two findings across four judges (three target, one negative control), three benchmarks, and three style axes.
5	First, the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects---complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---confirmed by seven statistical methods.
6	Whether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, Likert-only audits miss style-driven preferences that propagate through pairwise-based alignment pipelines.
7	Second, GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates per-axis confound structure consistent across three architectures---verbosity style-dominated, formality conditional, register judge-dependent---determining which estimates require conditional analysis and informing debiasing priorities.
8	
9	Style bias audits should adopt content-controlled pairwise comparison with per-axis decomposition, especially at moderate effect sizes ($n < 500$), where six experimental controls bound amplification risk (\S\ref{sec:amplification}; \citealt{jeong2025comparative}); existing Likert-based null findings may reflect measurement insensitivity rather than genuine absence of bias.
10	Our findings are established on English benchmarks with $n \leq 355$; validation on larger corpora, additional languages, and emerging judge architectures remains necessary, as does human evaluation of rewriting quality and stronger causal identification through randomized designs.
11

_source line 3604 · 2026-05-21T18:15:12.684058+00:00_

155	The verbosity axis provides the strongest and cleanest demonstration of the Blindspot.
156	All three target judges show consistent verbosity preference: GPT-4o 76.0\% ($p = 0.0001$), Qwen3-32B 77.3\% ($p < 0.0001$), and Llama-70B 73.5\% ($p = 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant for all three ($p = 0.403$, $0.179$, $0.752$).
157	Unlike formality, verbosity is style-dominated across all three judges: GEE decomposition confirms massive style effects (OR $= 8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$) while originality ORs are below 1 ($0.38$--$0.49$)---though Llama-70B's is the only significant one (OR $= 0.38$, $p = .020$)---indicating any artifact favors rewrites over originals rather than reflecting quality confounding (Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
158	Both rewriting directions favor verbose responses (concise$\to$verbose 90.7\%, verbose$\to$concise 80.6\%); controlling for length leaves the style OR unchanged ($<$2\%), and an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds evaluator-producer inflation to $22\%$ (Style OR $13.10 \to 10.17$; Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).
159	
160	\subsection{Register: Judge-Dependent Confound Structure}
161	\label{sec:negative_control}
162	
163	Register shows judge-dependent heterogeneity: Llama-70B BT 63.5\% ($p = 0.0014$), GPT-4o 57.0\% ($p = 0.04$), Qwen3-32B n.s.\ (55.3\%); all Likert n.s.
164	GEE confirms varying confound structure---Qwen3-32B originality-dominated, GPT-4o and Llama-70B mixed (Table~\ref{tab:gee})---classified as exploratory outside the primary Holm-Bonferroni family.
165	
166	\subsection{GEE Conditional Decomposition and Confound Structure}
167	\label{sec:direction}
168	
169	GEE conditional decomposition \citep{liang1986gee} with exchangeable working correlation and robust standard errors separates style from originality effects (Appendix~\ref{app:direction}; model diagnostics including correlation structure robustness and interaction tests in Appendix~\ref{app:gee_diagnostics}).

200	
201	The confound structure is consistent across all three target judges for verbosity (style-dominated: Style OR $8.2$--$13.1$, Orig OR $0.38$--$0.49$; only Llama-70B's reaches significance at $p = .020$) and formality (conditional); register is judge-dependent, from originality-dominated (Qwen3-32B) to mixed (GPT-4o, Llama-70B).
202	Cross-benchmark replication confirms formality bias across all six benchmark$\times$judge configurations (BT 63--73\%, all $p < 0.001$); Claude Sonnet 4 shows no bias (BT 56.2\%/54.1\%, n.s.), supporting discriminative validity.
203	The Blindspot manifests in eight of eleven bias-positive cells; three cross-benchmark exceptions (BT ${>}69\%$, Likert $d \geq 0.14$) confirm it is effect-size dependent---the same verbosity axis yields a Blindspot on LLMBar (BT 77.3\%, Likert n.s.) but not on AlpacaEval (BT 84.1\%, $d = 0.41$), demonstrating a measurement sensitivity boundary rather than a methodological artifact.
204	Same-style double-rewrite controls confirm a rewriting artifact (BT $P_{\text{orig}}$: $.08$--$.42$; Appendix~\ref{app:artifact_control}), absorbed by GEE's originality term.
205	Debiased prompting partially reduces but never eliminates bias across three architectures ($\Delta = {-}0.4$ to ${-}13.1$pp), with all five debiased estimates remaining significant (all $p < 0.001$), confirming that style preferences are embedded in judge representations.
206

_source line 3608 · 2026-05-21T18:15:14.844466+00:00_

18	Our contributions are as follows:
19	\begin{itemize}
20	    \item \textbf{A black-box controlled framework} for detecting per-axis style bias in LLM judges through style-controlled rewriting and position-controlled pairwise comparison (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
21	    \item \textbf{The Likert Blindspot:} Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects, following a severity spectrum---complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---across six judge$\times$axis configurations ($p < 0.001$ pairwise vs.\ $p > 0.17$ Likert). Whether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, the operational consequence is identical; seven statistical methods confirm the effect is structural (Section~\ref{sec:analysis}, Tables~\ref{tab:main_results}--\ref{tab:sensitivity}).
22	    \item \textbf{Per-axis confound structure:} GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates that verbosity bias is style-dominated across all three target judges (Style OR $8$--$13$, Orig OR ${<}1$), formality is conditional (significant style and originality effects), and register is judge-dependent (originality-dominated to mixed)---this structure determines which CSD estimates are directly interpretable (verbosity) versus requiring conditional interpretation (formality), and is invisible to aggregate metrics (Section~\ref{sec:direction}, Table~\ref{tab:gee}).

38	\section*{Limitations}
39	\label{sec:limitations}
40	
41	\paragraph{Quality--style confounding and construct validity.}
42	The NLI gate blocks content changes but cannot block quality perception shifts mediated by style itself---a fundamental identifiability limit of text-rewriting approaches.
43	Although the formality effect survives logistic adjustment for original preference ($\text{OR} = 2.32$, $p = 0.006$; Appendix~\ref{app:direction}), residual confounding is not fully eliminated.
44	An alternative interpretation of the Blindspot is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs: Likert captures absolute quality difference while pairwise captures ordinal preference. Under this view, the divergence reflects construct difference rather than measurement failure. The Comparative Trap \citep{jeong2025comparative} lends support to this alternative; six experimental controls bound this risk (\S\ref{sec:amplification}), though definitive separation would require human ground-truth ratings.
45	
46	\paragraph{Evaluator-producer bias and generalizability.}
47	Qwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge, raising self-preference concerns \citep{panickssery2024selfpreference}.
48	BT estimates are robust (cross-judge gap $+4.5$pp at $n = 355$), and both GPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B independently confirm the Blindspot across three architectures; however, cross-judge effect-size comparisons are not valid.
49	Register results carry the highest evaluator-producer risk---all register rewrites use the same Qwen3-32B rewriter without the cross-rewriter validation applied to formality and verbosity (Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep})---and should be treated as exploratory.
50	All experiments use LLMBar ($n = 100$); additional axes, larger benchmarks, and further judges (e.g., Gemini) are needed.
51	Whether findings transfer to settings with longer outputs, domain-specific evaluation, or human judges requires further investigation.
52	Furthermore, our per-axis decompositi

### Tool result

_source line 3612 · 2026-05-21T18:15:28.474991+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM judges are widely deployed for evaluation and alignment, yet style features alone explain judge scores with near-perfect correlation.
3	We introduce Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), the first framework combining NLI-gated content control, per-axis style decomposition, and position-controlled pairwise comparison to systematically quantify style bias in LLM judges.
4	Across three target judges (GPT-4o, Qwen3-32B, Llama-3.3-70B) and one negative control (Claude Sonnet 4), three benchmarks, and three style axes, CSD demonstrates the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects (BT 63--84\%, $p < 0.001$), following a severity spectrum---complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$), exceeding 60\% attenuation at stronger effects---confirmed by seven statistical methods.
5	GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates a per-axis confound structure consistent across all three target judges: verbosity bias is style-dominated, formality exhibits a conditional effect moderated by content quality changes, and register is judge-dependent (originality-dominated to mixed)---determining which estimates are directly interpretable and which require conditional analysis.
6	A fourth judge (Claude Sonnet 4) detects no bias on either axis, supporting discriminative validity.
7	We identify two candidate interpretations---measurement attenuation and construct divergence---and show that both yield the same operational conclusion: Likert-only evaluation systematically misses style-driven preferences that propagate through pairwise-based alignment pipelines.
8	\end{abstract}
9

173	\section{Claude Sonnet 4 Equivalence Testing}
174	\label{app:claude_tost}
175	
176	We failed to detect style bias for Claude at the current sample size ($n = 87$; BT $p = 0.091$, $p = 0.284$).
177	Formal equivalence testing \citep[TOST;][]{lakens2017equivalence} at $\Delta = 10$pp is underpowered at $n = 87$; at $\Delta = 15$pp both axes confirm equivalence, bounding any Claude style preference to ${<}15$pp---substantively distinct from GPT-4o ($13$--$23$pp) and Qwen3-32B ($17$--$27$pp).
178	The $\Delta = 15$pp margin represents a conservative upper bound: it exceeds all observed negative-control deviations from 50\% (Claude: $+6.2$/$+4.1$pp) while remaining below the smallest confirmed bias ($+13$pp for GPT-4o formality), ensuring the equivalence zone excludes effects we would classify as genuine bias.
179	
180	\section{Bradley-Terry Goodness of Fit and Stratified Analysis}
181	\label{app:bt_gof}

**Must-Fix（必须修）：**

1. **R7-W2 Register 分类矛盾**：Section 4.1 说 primary family 含 register（暗示 8 tests），但 Table 1 用 †_e 标记 register 为 exploratory。**统一为 6-test primary family**：
   - Section 4.1 明确写 "primary family of 6 tests (formality × 3 judges + verbosity × 3 judges)"
   - 确认 Table 1 register 行的 †_e 标记正确
   - 检查全文其他提到 "primary family" / Holm-Bonferroni 校正的地方是否一致

2. **R5-SF1 "first framework" overclaim**：将 Abstract/Intro 中所有 "the first framework" 改为 "a unified framework" 或 "a framework that integrates"。搜索全文所有 "the first" / "first framework" / "first systematic" 实例，逐一改掉。

3. **RD2-SF1 Evaluator-producer confound**：
   - Limitations 中加强 register 轴 caveat：明确说 register cross-rewriter validation 缺失，register 结论 tentative
   - Section 5.4 或正文中，在提到 cross-rewriter 22% Style OR 下降时，加一句 producer-corrected effect range（例如 "After correcting for rewriter identity, Style OR ranges from 10.2 to 13.1 across judges"——用论文已有数据）

**Should-Fix：**

4. **R5-M3 TOST 语言**：搜索全文所有关于 Claude negative control 的描述，将 "equivalence confirmed" / "no bias" / "unbiased" 改为 "failed to detect bias at Δ=15pp precision" 或 "TOST provides upper bound on undetected bias"。

5. **R7-W4 NLI FNR 循环性**：在 Section 5.1 或 NLI validation 相关段落，加一句：明确标注 8.4% FNR 来自与 rewriter 相同的 Qwen3-32B 模型，引导读者参考 GPT-4o cross-validation（precision 92.1%/82.6%）作为独立验证。

6. **R7-W5 Verbosity 17.4% FPR**：在 Section 5.1 加入 verbosity direction-aware gate GPT-4o validation 17.4% FPR 数字，简短讨论其对 BT 估计的 potential bias（GEE Orig OR < 1 部分吸收）。

7. **R3-M3 Abstract 过密**：将 Abstract 中的具体统计数字（Style OR 括号数字等）移到 Introduction 首段引出，Abstract 保留定性结论和核心 message。目标：Abstract 不超过 5-6 句，无括号内统计数据。

8. **R5-SF6 Measurement vs construct balance**：微调 Abstract 用词——如果当前 Abstract 侧重 "measurement failure" 叙事，加入一句平衡 construct difference 解释（"whether this reflects measurement attenuation or genuine construct divergence"）。

9. **R5-MF1 Conclusion amplification caveat**：检查 Conclusion 中是否仍有 "should adopt pairwise" 类无条件建议。如有，改为条件式：如 "pairwise comparison offers detection advantages, provided that amplification controls (position balancing, content filtering) are in place"。

10. **R7-REF1 新引用**：在 Section 5.2 或 Related Work 引用 **Tripathi et al. (2025) "Pairwise or Pointwise?"** 和 **Li et al. (2026) "Grading Scale Impact"**。注意：这两篇引用需要 Main Agent 先验证真实性，bib 条目会后续提供。如果修改时尚无 bib 条目，先用 `\textcolor{red}{[CITE]}` 占位。

68	\subsection{NLI Gate Validation}
69	\label{sec:nli_validation}
70	
71	\paragraph{False negative rate.}
72	We validate all 100 NLI decisions against an LLM annotator (Qwen3-32B) prompted to judge whether semantic content is preserved.
73	The two systems agree on 91 of 100 pairs (Gwet's AC1 $= 0.89$; Cohen's $\kappa = 0.43$ is depressed by prevalence skew \citep{feinstein1990kappa}).
74	Of the 12 rejected pairs, 8 are judged content-preserving by the LLM annotator, yielding an overall false negative rate of $8/95 = 8.4\%$.
75	Three robustness checks confirm these do not compromise conclusions: BT stable across 11 NLI thresholds from 0.50 to 0.98 (${\leq}3$pp variation across all four judge$\times$axis configurations; \S\ref{sec:nli_threshold}), no covariate differences between retained and discarded pairs (all $p > 0.12$; \S\ref{sec:consistency}), and no directional bias in false negatives (Fisher $p = 0.35$).
76	The NLI gate operates as a precision-oriented conservative filter (precision $= 98.9\%$); verbosity-rejected pairs have larger edit distances (Cliff's $\delta = +0.45$, $p < 0.001$), making estimates lower bounds.
77	Full-scale GPT-4o validation on all 200 NLI gate decisions yields 92.1\% pass precision for formality and 82.6\% for verbosity, with the lower verbosity figure reflecting the permissiveness of direction-aware gating (17.4\% false positives vs.\ 7.9\% for the strict bidirectional formality gate---a trade-off for the higher pass rate that enables adequate sample sizes).
78	
79	\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.}
80	\label{sec:nli_threshold}
81	A continuous sweep across 11 NLI thresholds (0.50--0.98) confirms that BT estimates are robust to threshold choice: all 44 judge$\times$axis$\times$threshold combinations are significant, with maximum variation ${\leq}3$pp within each configuration (Appendix~\ref{app:nli_sweep}).

85	\subsection{Measurement Sensitivity: Likert vs.\ Pairwise}
86	\label{sec:likert_pairwise}
87	
88	\paragraph{Head-to-head comparison with RATE.}
89	Applying RATE's Likert ATE methodology \citep{reber2025rate} to our formality style-transformed pairs yields Cohen's $d = 0.10$, $p = 0.33$ (non-significant, $n = 88$); pairwise comparison on identical data reveals a 63.0\% formal preference ($p = 0.0004$, Bradley-Terry on all 173 comparisons), confirmed by a consistency-filtered sign test (73.9\%, $p = 0.006$, $n = 46$ consistent pairs).
90	This is the Likert Blindspot: the same data, the same style intervention, two measurement instruments---one detects bias, the other does not.
91	A $4\times$ scaled replication ($n = 355$) distinguishes measurement attenuation from insufficient power: the Likert effect size shrinks to $d = {-}0.025$ (near zero) while BT remains stable at $67.5\%$ ($p < 0.0001$)---if the gap were merely statistical power, $d$ would remain non-zero at larger $n$.
92	Bootstrap subsampling across seven sample sizes confirms this pattern: $d$ remains flat near zero ($d_{\text{median}} = {-}0.017$) as CIs narrow from ${\pm}0.3$ to ${\pm}0.1$, never excluding zero (Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_attenuation}).
93	Attenuation follows a severity spectrum.
94	At moderate bias (BT 63--67\%), it is complete: $d$ converges to ${\approx}0$ even at $n = 355$, rendering the bias invisible to any Likert-based test.
95	At stronger effects (BT ${>}70\%$), Likert partially detects but severely attenuates: MT-Bench formality yields Likert $d \approx 0.15$ for BT ${\sim}70\%$ effects---${>}60\%$ signal compression---explaining why three of eleven bias-positive cells escape the Blindspot (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, $\ddagger$ rows).
96	
97	The two frameworks are complementary: RATE measures aggregate quality effects via Likert ATE; CSD targets per-axis style preference via forced-choice comparison, which eliminates ties and is more sensitive to ordinal preferences.
98	The

```json
{
```
```json
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_5/projects/causal_style_decomp_judge/docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex",
  "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM judges are widely deployed for evaluation and alignment, yet style features alone explain judge scores with near-perfect correlation.\nWe introduce Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a unified framework combining NLI-gated content control, per-axis style decomposition, and position-controlled pairwise comparison to systematically quantify style bias in LLM judges.\nAcross three target judges, one negative control, three benchmarks, and three style axes, CSD demonstrates the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects, following a severity spectrum from complete attenuation at moderate bias to over 60\\% signal loss at stronger effects, confirmed by seven statistical methods.\nGEE conditional decomposition demonstrates a per-axis confound structure consistent across all three target judges: verbosity bias is style-dominated, formality exhibits a conditional effect moderated by content quality, and register is judge-dependent---determining which estimates are directly interpretable and which require conditional analysis.\nA negative-control judge shows no detectable bias on either primary axis, supporting discriminative validity.\nWhether this divergence reflects measurement attenuation or genuine construct divergence, both interpretations yield the same operational conclusion: Likert-only evaluation systematically misses style-driven preferences that propagate through pairwise-based alignment pipelines.\n\\end{abstract}\n"
}
```

</details>

```json
  "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a black-box framework yielding two findings across four judges, three benchmarks, and three style axes.\nFirst, the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects---complete at moderate bias ($d \\to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---confirmed by seven statistical methods.\nWhether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, Likert-only audits miss style-driven preferences that propagate through alignment pipelines.\nSecond, GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates per-axis confound structure consistent across three architectures---verbosity style-dominated, formality conditional, register judge-dependent---determining which estimates require conditional analysis and informing debiasing priorities.\n\nPairwise comparison offers superior detection sensitivity for style bias, provided that amplification controls---position balancing, content filtering, and per-axis decomposition---are in place (\\S\\ref{sec:amplification}); existing Likert-based null findings may reflect measurement insensitivity rather than genuine absence of bias.\nOur findings are established on English benchmarks with $n \\leq 355$; validation on larger corpora, additional languages, and emerging judge architectures remains necessary.\n"
}
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 3662 · 2026-05-21T21:41:52.050934+00:00_

20	    \item \textbf{A black-box controlled framework} for detecting per-axis style bias in LLM judges through style-controlled rewriting and position-controlled pairwise comparison (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
21	    \item \textbf{The Likert Blindspot:} Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects, following a severity spectrum---complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---across six judge$\times$axis configurations ($p < 0.001$ pairwise vs.\ $p > 0.17$ Likert). Whether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, the operational consequence is identical; seven statistical methods confirm the effect is structural (Section~\ref{sec:analysis}, Tables~\ref{tab:main_results}--\ref{tab:sensitivity}).
22	    \item \textbf{Per-axis confound structure:} GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates that verbosity bias is style-dominated across all three target judges (Style OR $8$--$13$, Orig OR ${<}1$), formality is conditional (significant style and originality effects), and register is judge-dependent (originality-dominated to mixed)---this structure determines which CSD estimates are directly interpretable (verbosity) versus requiring conditional interpretation (formality), and is invisible to aggregate metrics (Section~\ref{sec:direction}, Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
23	    \item \textbf{Practical implications:} style bias audits require pairwise comparison \emph{and} per-axis decomposition; aggregate metrics and Likert-based findings may systematically underestimate bias or conflate distinct confound structures (Section~\ref{sec:conclusion}).
24	\end{itemize}

[Director 指令] [Patch 补充] Reviewer 独立审视后的关键修正 + 数据补充。请与上一条指令合并执行。

**⚠️ MF1 Evaluator-producer：修正修复方向**

Reviewer 指出我之前的指令方向有误。关键区分：
- **Qwen3-32B judge**：rewriter 也是 Qwen3-32B → 存在 evaluator-producer overlap → cross-rewriter test 已覆盖，22% OR drop 是 Qwen3-specific bounding
- **GPT-4o / Llama-70B judge**：rewriter 是 Qwen3-32B ≠ judge → **不存在 evaluator-producer overlap** → self-preference bias (Panickssery 2024) 不适用

**修复方向调整**：不要笼统承认所有 judge 都有问题。在 §5.4 或 Limitations 明确写：
> "Evaluator-producer overlap exists only for Qwen3-32B, which serves as both rewriter and judge; GPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B use a separate rewriter (Qwen3-32B) and are thus not affected by self-preference bias. For the Qwen3 configuration, cross-rewriter validation bounds the inflation at 22% Style OR reduction (13.10→10.17)."

**不要**给 GPT-4o/Llama 的 OR 估计标注 ±22%。22% 是 Qwen3-specific 的。

---

**⚠️ SF8 引用修正**

1. **Li et al. (2026) 已在论文中引用**（Intro line 7, Related Work lines 30-31）。不需要再加。

2. **Tripathi et al. (2025) 需小心 framing**。该论文核心发现是 pairwise 更易被 distractor 操纵（flip rate 35% vs pointwise 9%），结论倾向 absolute scoring 更 robust。这与 CSD 的 pairwise 检测优势立场有张力。

   推荐引用位置和 framing：在 §5.2 construct divergence 段落：
   > "Tripathi et al. (2025) independently confirm that pairwise and pointwise protocols exhibit distinct sensitivity profiles, with pairwise preferences being more volatile—consistent with our construct divergence interpretation and reinforcing the need to understand which measurement mode underlies downstream applications."

   Bib 条目：
   ```bibtex
   @article{tripathi2025pairwise,
     title={Pairwise or Pointwise? Evaluating Feedback Protocols for Bias in LLM-Based Evaluation},
     author={Tripathi, Tuhina and Wadhwa, Manya and Durrett, Greg and Niekum, Scott},
     journal={arXiv preprint arXiv:2504.14716},
     year={2025}
   }
   ```

---

129	
130	Figure~\ref{fig:power_curve} plots detection probability curves at observed effect magnitudes (not post-hoc power; cf.\ \citealt{hoenig2001abuse}): Likert requires ${\approx}4.5\times$ more data to detect the same bias.
131	Formal power analysis across all six judge$\times$axis cells confirms: Likert would require $4$--$206\times$ the current sample size ($336$--$27{,}159$ pairs) to detect effects that BT identifies at $p < 0.001$ with $n \leq 100$; for formality, the required samples exceed $18{,}000$ pairs---orders of magnitude beyond typical evaluation budgets (Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}).
132	\paragraph{Measurement attenuation vs.\ construct divergence.}
133	An equally plausible interpretation is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs---absolute quality vs.\ ordinal preference---making the divergence a construct difference rather than measurement failure.
134	Both interpretations lead to the same conclusion: Likert audits miss style preferences that drive pairwise-based alignment pipelines (RLHF, DPO).
135	
136	\paragraph{Detection vs.\ amplification.}
137	\label{sec:amplification}
138	\citet{jeong2025comparative} show that pairwise comparison amplifies style biases (the Comparative Trap).
139	While format-level amplification of micro-preferences cannot be fully excluded, six independent controls bound this risk: (i)~NLI gate ablation reverses NLI-fail pairs to BT 41.7\% (Appendix~\ref{app:nli_ablation}); (ii)~Claude Sonnet 4 shows no bias with the identical pairwise format (BT 56.2\%/54.1\%, n.s.); (iii)~cross-axis BT variation (register 55\% $<$ formality 67\% $<$ verbosity 77\%) is inconsistent with uniform format amplification; (iv)~rewriting artifact absorbed by GEE decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:artifact_control}); (v)~debiased prompting partially reduces bias (verbosity $-13.1$pp; formality $-5.7$/$-0.4$pp); (vi)~three independent architectures all confirm the Blindsp

_source line 3686 · 2026-05-21T21:44:21.710527+00:00_

155	\label{sec:cross_axis}
156	
157	The verbosity axis provides the strongest and cleanest demonstration of the Blindspot.
158	All three target judges show consistent verbosity preference: GPT-4o 76.0\% ($p = 0.0001$), Qwen3-32B 77.3\% ($p < 0.0001$), and Llama-70B 73.5\% ($p = 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant for all three ($p = 0.403$, $0.179$, $0.752$).
159	Unlike formality, verbosity is style-dominated across all three judges: GEE decomposition confirms massive style effects (OR $= 8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$) while originality ORs are below 1 ($0.38$--$0.49$)---though Llama-70B's is the only significant one (OR $= 0.38$, $p = .020$)---indicating any artifact favors rewrites over originals rather than reflecting quality confounding (Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
160	Both rewriting directions favor verbose responses (concise$\to$verbose 90.7\%, verbose$\to$concise 80.6\%); controlling for length leaves the style OR unchanged ($<$2\%), and an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds evaluator-producer inflation to $22\%$ (Style OR $13.10 \to 10.17$; Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}), establishing a producer-corrected verbosity Style OR range of $10.2$--$13.1$.
161	
162	\subsection{Register: Judge-Dependent Confound Structure}
163	\label{sec:negative_control}
164	
165	Register shows judge-dependent heterogeneity: Llama-70B BT 63.5\% ($p = 0.0014$), GPT-4o 57.0\% ($p = 0.04$), Qwen3-32B n.s.\ (55.3\%); all Likert n.s.

46	\paragraph{Evaluator-producer bias and generalizability.}
47	Qwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge, raising self-preference concerns \citep{panickssery2024selfpreference}.
48	BT estimates are robust (cross-judge gap $+4.5$pp at $n = 355$), and both GPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B independently confirm the Blindspot across three architectures; however, cross-judge effect-size comparisons are not valid.
49	Register results carry the highest evaluator-producer risk---all register rewrites use the same Qwen3-32B rewriter without the cross-rewriter validation applied to formality and verbosity (Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep})---and register conclusions remain tentative pending independent rewriter validation.
50	All experiments use LLMBar ($n = 100$); additional axes, larger benchmarks, and further judges (e.g., Gemini) are needed.
51	Whether findings transfer to settings with longer outputs, domain-specific evaluation, or human judges requires further investigation.
52	Furthermore, our per-axis decomposition treats each style axis independently; in practice, axes may interact (e.g., formality shifts may co-vary with vocabulary sophistication or register), and such axis-specific confounds are not isolated by the current design.
53	
54	\paragraph{Validation methodology and scale design.}
55	NLI validation uses Qwen3-32B as annotator (GPT-4o cross-validation on all 200 pairs confirms 92.1\% formality / 82.6\% verbosity pass precision); human evaluation of both original and rewritten texts would strengthen content-preservation claims.

38	\section*{Limitations}
39	\label{sec:limitations}
40	
41	\paragraph{Quality--style confounding and construct validity.}
42	The NLI gate blocks content changes but cannot block quality perception shifts mediated by style itself---a fundamental identifiability limit of text-rewriting approaches.
43	Although the formality effect survives logistic adjustment for original preference ($\text{OR} = 2.32$, $p = 0.006$; Appendix~\ref{app:direction}), residual confounding is not fully eliminated.
44	An alternative interpretation of the Blindspot is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs: Likert captures absolute quality difference while pairwise captures ordinal preference. Under this view, the divergence reflects construct difference rather than measurement failure. The Comparative Trap \citep{jeong2025comparative} lends support to this alternative; six experimental controls bound this risk (\S\ref{sec:amplification}), though definitive separation would require human ground-truth ratings.
45	
46	\paragraph{Evaluator-producer bias and generalizability.}
47	Qwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge, raising self-preference concerns \citep{panickssery2024selfpreference}.
48	BT estimates are robust (cross-judge gap $+4.5$pp at $n = 355$), and both GPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B independently confirm the Blindspot across three architectures; however, cross-judge effect-size comparisons are not valid.
49	Register results carry the highest evaluator-producer risk---all register rewrites use the same Qwen3-32B rewriter without the cross-rewriter validation applied to formality and verbosity (Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep})---and register conclusions remain tentative pending independent rewriter validation.
50	All experiments use LLMBar ($n = 100$); additional axes, larger benchmarks, and further judges (e.g., Gemini) are needed.

18	\citet{preston2000scale} show that scale granularity affects reliability, while \citet{krosnick1999survey} documents satisficing behavior that may compound the tie inflation we observe.
19	\citet{decoster2011median} show that artificial categorization of continuous variables discards variance and reduces statistical power, directly relevant to the Likert Blindspot mechanism.
20	Within NLP, \citet{amidei2019likert} review the use of rating and Likert scales in NLG evaluation, identifying inconsistencies in scale design and reporting that compromise cross-study comparability.
21	\citet{clark2021human} demonstrate systematic flaws in human evaluation; \citet{celikyilmaz2020evaluation} survey evaluation methods, and \citet{vanderlee2019best,vanderlee2021human} establish best practices for human NLG evaluation.
22	\citet{kiritchenko2017bws} and \citet{novikova2018rankme} confirm that forced-choice methods (best-worst scaling, ranking) detect quality differences that rating scales miss; \citet{louviere2015bws} provide the theoretical foundation.
23	Our contribution provides a systematic quantification of this gap for LLM judge style bias via controlled per-axis style decomposition, identifying the two-layer mechanism (tie inflation plus residual signal attenuation) that explains it \textcolor{red}{[cf.\ CITE: Tripathi et al., 2025; Li et al., 2026]}.
24	
25	\paragraph{Style Bias Detection and Mitigation}
26	The foundational LLM-as-judge framework was established by \citet{zheng2023judging}, who introduced MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena and documented position bias in pairwise evaluation.
27	\citet{wang2023faireval} further showed that preference rankings can be manipulated by swapping option order, proposing a win/tie/lose annotation scheme, and \citet{shi2024judging} systematically study position bias across 15 judges, showing it varies by model and quality gap.

130	Figure~\ref{fig:power_curve} plots detection probability curves at observed effect magnitudes (not post-hoc power; cf.\ \citealt{hoenig2001abuse}): Likert requires ${\approx}4.5\times$ more data to detect the same bias.
131	Formal power analysis across all six judge$\times$axis cells confirms: Likert would require $4$--$206\times$ the current sample size ($336$--$27{,}159$ pairs) to detect effects that BT identifies at $p < 0.001$ with $n \leq 100$; for formality, the required samples exceed $18{,}000$ pairs---orders of magnitude beyond typical evaluation budgets (Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}).
132	\paragraph{Measurement attenuation vs.\ construct divergence.}
133	An equally plausible interpretation is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs---absolute quality vs.\ ordinal preference---making the divergence a construct difference rather than measurement failure.
134	Both interpretations lead to the same conclusion: Likert audits miss style preferences that drive pairwise-based alignment pipelines (RLHF, DPO).
135

```json
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_5/projects/causal_style_decomp_judge/docs/paper/main.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Evaluator-producer bias and generalizability.}\nQwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge, raising self-preference concerns \\citep{panickssery2024selfpreference}.\nBT estimates are robust (cross-judge gap $+4.5$pp at $n = 355$), and both GPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B independently confirm the Blindspot across three architectures; however, cross-judge effect-size comparisons are not valid.\nRegister results carry the highest evaluator-producer risk---all register rewrites use the same Qwen3-32B rewriter without the cross-rewriter validation applied to formality and verbosity (Appendix~\\ref{app:rewriter_indep})---and register conclusions remain tentative pending independent rewriter validation.\nAll experiments use LLMBar ($n = 100$); additional axes, larger benchmarks, and further judges (e.g., Gemini) are needed.\nWhether findings transfer to settings with longer outputs, domain-specific evaluation, or human judges requires further investigation.\nFurthermore, our per-axis decomposition treats each style axis independently; in practice, axes may interact (e.g., formality shifts may co-vary with vocabulary sophistication or register), and such axis-specific confounds are not isolated by the current design.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Evaluator-producer bias and generalizability.}\nQwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge, raising self-preference concerns \\citep{panickssery2024selfpreference}; an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds this to a 22\\% Style OR reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).\nGPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B use a separate rewriter and are not subject to evaluator-producer overlap; both independently confirm the Blindspot across three architectures, though cross-judge effect-size comparisons are not valid.\nRegister results carry the highest evaluator-producer risk---all register rewrites use the same Qwen3-32B rewriter without the cross-rewriter validation applied to formality and verbosity---and register conclusions remain tentative pending independent rewriter validation.\nAll experiments use LLMBar ($n = 100$); additional axes, larger benchmarks, and further judges (e.g., Gemini) are needed.\nOur per-axis decomposition treats each style axis independently; in practice, axes may interact (e.g., formality shifts may co-vary with register), and such confounds are not isolated by the current design."
}
```

</details>

```json
    "old_string": "\\paragraph{Evaluator-producer bias and generalizability.}\nQwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge, raising self-preference concerns \\citep{panickssery2024selfpreference}.\nBT estimates are robust (cross-judge gap $+4.5$pp at $n = 355$), and both GPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B independently confirm the Blindspot across three architectures; however, cross-judge effect-size comparisons are not valid.\nRegister results carry the highest evaluator-producer risk---all register rewrites use the same Qwen3-32B rewriter without the cross-rewriter validation applied to formality and verbosity (Appendix~\\ref{app:rewriter_indep})---and register conclusions remain tentative pending independent rewriter validation.\nAll experiments use LLMBar ($n = 100$); additional axes, larger benchmarks, and further judges (e.g., Gemini) are needed.\nWhether findings transfer to settings with longer outputs, domain-specific evaluation, or human judges requires further investigation.\nFurthermore, our per-axis decomposition treats each style axis independently; in practice, axes may interact (e.g., formality shifts may co-vary with vocabulary sophistication or register), and such axis-specific confounds are not isolated by the current design.",
    "new_string": "\\paragraph{Evaluator-producer bias and generalizability.}\nQwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge, raising self-preference concerns \\citep{panickssery2024selfpreference}; an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds this to a 22\\% Style OR reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).\nGPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B use a separate rewriter and are not subject to evaluator-producer overlap; both independently confirm the Blindspot across three architectures, though cross-judge effect-size comparisons are not valid.\nRegister results carry the highest evaluator-producer risk---all register rewrites use the same Qwen3-32B rewriter without the cross-rewriter validation applied to formality and verbosity---and register conclusions remain tentative pending independent rewriter validation.\nAll experiments use LLMBar ($n = 100$); additional axes, larger benchmarks, and further judges (e.g., Gemini) are needed.\nOur per-axis decomposition treats each style axis independently; in practice, axes may interact (e.g., formality shifts may co-vary with register), and such confounds are not isolated by the current design."

}
```
