{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_5/projects/causal_style_decomp_judge/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Measurement attenuation vs.\\ construct divergence.}\nAn equally plausible interpretation is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs---absolute quality vs.\\ ordinal preference---making the divergence a construct difference rather than measurement failure.\nBoth interpretations lead to the same conclusion: Likert audits miss style preferences that drive pairwise-based alignment pipelines (RLHF, DPO).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Measurement attenuation vs.\\ construct divergence.}\nAn equally plausible interpretation is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs---absolute quality vs.\\ ordinal preference---making the divergence a construct difference rather than measurement failure.\n\\citet{tripathi2025pairwise} independently confirm that pairwise and pointwise protocols exhibit distinct sensitivity profiles, with pairwise preferences being more volatile---consistent with this construct divergence interpretation.\nBoth interpretations lead to the same conclusion: Likert audits miss style preferences that drive pairwise-based alignment pipelines (RLHF, DPO)."
}
```

</details>

    "new_string": "\\paragraph{Measurement attenuation vs.\\ construct divergence.}\nAn equally plausible interpretation is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs---absolute quality vs.\\ ordinal preference---making the divergence a construct difference rather than measurement failure.\n\\citet{tripathi2025pairwise} independently confirm that pairwise and pointwise protocols exhibit distinct sensitivity profiles, with pairwise preferences being more volatile---consistent with this construct divergence interpretation.\nBoth interpretations lead to the same conclusion: Likert audits miss style preferences that drive pairwise-based alignment pipelines (RLHF, DPO)."

130	Figure~\ref{fig:power_curve} plots detection probability curves at observed effect magnitudes (not post-hoc power; cf.\ \citealt{hoenig2001abuse}): Likert requires ${\approx}4.5\times$ more data to detect the same bias.
131	Formal power analysis across all six judge$\times$axis cells confirms: Likert would require $4$--$206\times$ the current sample size ($336$--$27{,}159$ pairs) to detect effects that BT identifies at $p < 0.001$ with $n \leq 100$; for formality, the required samples exceed $18{,}000$ pairs---orders of magnitude beyond typical evaluation budgets (Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}).
132	\paragraph{Measurement attenuation vs.\ construct divergence.}
133	An equally plausible interpretation is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs---absolute quality vs.\ ordinal preference---making the divergence a construct difference rather than measurement failure.
134	\citet{tripathi2025pairwise} independently confirm that pairwise and pointwise protocols exhibit distinct sensitivity profiles, with pairwise preferences being more volatile---consistent with this construct divergence interpretation.
135	Both interpretations lead to the same conclusion: Likert audits miss style preferences that drive pairwise-based alignment pipelines (RLHF, DPO).
136	
137	\paragraph{Detection vs.\ amplification.}
138	\label{sec:amplification}
139	\citet{jeong2025comparative} show that pairwise comparison amplifies style biases (the Comparative Trap).
140	While format-level amplification of micro-preferences cannot be fully excluded, six independent controls bound this risk: (i)~NLI gate ablation reverses NLI-fail pairs to BT 41.7\% (Appendix~\ref{app:nli_ablation}); (ii)~Claude Sonnet 4 shows no bias with the identical pairwise format (BT 56.2\%/54.1\%, n.s.); (iii)~cross-axis BT variation (register 55\% $<$ formality 67\% $<$ verbosity 77\%

38	\section*{Limitations}
39	\label{sec:limitations}
40	
41	\paragraph{Quality--style confounding and construct validity.}
42	The NLI gate blocks content changes but cannot block quality perception shifts mediated by style itself---a fundamental identifiability limit of text-rewriting approaches.
43	Although the formality effect survives logistic adjustment for original preference ($\text{OR} = 2.32$, $p = 0.006$; Appendix~\ref{app:direction}), residual confounding is not fully eliminated.
44	An alternative interpretation of the Blindspot is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs: Likert captures absolute quality difference while pairwise captures ordinal preference. Under this view, the divergence reflects construct difference rather than measurement failure. The Comparative Trap \citep{jeong2025comparative} lends support to this alternative; six experimental controls bound this risk (\S\ref{sec:amplification}), though definitive separation would require human ground-truth ratings.
45	
46	\paragraph{Evaluator-producer bias and generalizability.}
47	Qwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge, raising self-preference concerns \citep{panickssery2024selfpreference}; an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds this to a 22\% Style OR reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).
48	GPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B use a separate rewriter and are not subject to evaluator-producer overlap; both independently confirm the Blindspot across three architectures, though cross-judge effect-size comparisons are not valid.
49	Register results carry the highest evaluator-producer risk---all register rewrites use the same Qwen3-32B rewriter without the cross-rewriter validation applied to formality and verbosity---and register conclusions remain tentative pending independent rewriter validation.
50	All experiments use LLMBar ($n = 100$); additional axes, larger benchmarks, and further judges (e.g., Gemini) are needed.
51	Our per-axis decomposition treats each style axis ind

20	Within NLP, \citet{amidei2019likert} review the use of rating and Likert scales in NLG evaluation, identifying inconsistencies in scale design and reporting that compromise cross-study comparability.
21	\citet{clark2021human} demonstrate systematic flaws in human evaluation; \citet{celikyilmaz2020evaluation} survey evaluation methods, and \citet{vanderlee2019best,vanderlee2021human} establish best practices for human NLG evaluation.
22	\citet{kiritchenko2017bws} and \citet{novikova2018rankme} confirm that forced-choice methods (best-worst scaling, ranking) detect quality differences that rating scales miss; \citet{louviere2015bws} provide the theoretical foundation.
23	Our contribution provides a systematic quantification of this gap for LLM judge style bias via controlled per-axis style decomposition, identifying the two-layer mechanism (tie inflation plus residual signal attenuation) that explains it.
24

[Director 指令] [Patch] 两项新修改：Figure 1 Case Study + 模型版本 Justification

**1. Figure 1 "Blindspot in Action" Case Study**

从现有 scored data 中挑选 1-2 个最 compelling 的 formality pairs，制作 "Blindspot in Action" 示例。格式：

```
Instruction: [实际 instruction]
Response A (Casual): [前2-3句]
Response B (Formal): [前2-3句]

NLI Gate: PASS (score: 0.97)  — content preserved

GPT-4o Likert:  A = 8/10, B = 8/10  (TIE → no bias detected)
GPT-4o Pairwise: B wins (both orderings) → formal preference detected
```

**位置选择**（二选一，你判断）：
- 方案 A：替换现有 Figure 1（pipeline diagram 移到 §3）
- 方案 B：作为 §1 Introduction 开头的 "Motivating Example"（pipeline diagram 保留为 Figure 2）

推荐方案 B——pipeline diagram 仍有价值，motivating example 放在 Intro 开头让 reviewer 第一时间理解问题。

**数据来源**：读取 `/root/causal_style_decomp_judge/data/` 下的 scored formality pairs，挑选满足以下条件的 pair：
- NLI score ≥ 0.95（高置信内容保留）
- Likert 分数相近（差 ≤1，最好 tie）
- Pairwise 强偏好（两个方向都选同一个）
- Instruction 简短易懂

如果无法直接读取服务器数据，请告知我让 Main Agent 提供候选 examples。

**2. §4 模型版本 Justification（~200 words）**

在 §4 Experimental Setup 中添加一段模型选择说明：

> "**Model Selection.** Our target judges span three independent architectures: GPT-4o (2024-08-06), the dominant LLM judge powering AlpacaEval and MT-Bench leaderboards during 2024--2025; Qwen3-32B-Instruct, used as both rewriter and judge for methodological consistency within the Qwen3 generation; and Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct, the latest dense model in the Llama family (Llama~4 adopts a MoE architecture, introducing an additional confound). Claude Sonnet~4 serves as negative control. While newer models have since been released for some families, our framework applies to any black-box judge, and the Blindspot phenomenon is confirmed across three independent architectures spanning both proprietary and open-weight models. We pin model versions for reproducibility; whether the Blindspot persists in successor architectures is an empirical question CSD can readily test."

如果 Gemini 3.5 Flash 实验完成且为 positive，后续会追加一句 "We additionally test Gemini-3.5-Flash (released May 19, 2026) to verify the Blindspot persists in the most recent models."

完成后编译确认 ≤8 页。

8	We designate a primary family of six hypothesis tests (three judges $\times$ two axes: formality and verbosity) and control the family-wise error rate with Holm-Bonferroni correction at $\alpha = 0.05$.
9	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports both raw and adjusted $p$-values.

11	Exploratory analyses are reported with uncorrected $p$-values and interpreted as hypothesis-generating; confirmatory analyses test specific hypotheses about pipeline validity and are evaluated at the nominal $\alpha = 0.05$ level without additional correction, as they address orthogonal questions about measurement reliability rather than the primary style-bias family.

88	\label{sec:likert_pairwise}
89	
90	\paragraph{Head-to-head comparison with RATE.}
91	Applying RATE's Likert ATE methodology \citep{reber2025rate} to our formality style-transformed pairs yields Cohen's $d = 0.10$, $p = 0.33$ (non-significant, $n = 88$); pairwise comparison on identical data reveals a 63.0\% formal preference ($p = 0.0004$, Bradley-Terry on all 173 comparisons), confirmed by a consistency-filtered sign test (73.9\%, $p = 0.006$, $n = 46$ consistent pairs).
92	This is the Likert Blindspot: the same data, the same style intervention, two measurement instruments---one detects bias, the other does not.
93	A $4\times$ scaled replication ($n = 355$) distinguishes measurement attenuation from insufficient power: the Likert effect size shrinks to $d = {-}0.025$ (near zero) while BT remains stable at $67.5\%$ ($p < 0.0001$)---if the gap were merely statistical power, $d$ would remain non-zero at larger $n$.
94	Bootstrap subsampling across seven sample sizes confirms this pattern: $d$ remains flat near zero ($d_{\text{median}} = {-}0.017$) as CIs narrow from ${\pm}0.3$ to ${\pm}0.1$, never excluding zero (Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_attenuation}).
95	Attenuation follows a severity spectrum.
96	At moderate bias (BT 63--67\%), it is complete: $d$ converges to ${\approx}0$ even at $n = 355$, rendering the bias invisible to any Likert-based test.
97	At stronger effects (BT ${>}70\%$), Likert partially detects but severely attenuates: MT-Bench formality yields Likert $d \approx 0.15$ for BT ${\sim}70\%$ effects---${>}60\%$ signal compression---explaining why three of eleven bias-positive cells escape the Blindspot (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, $\ddagger$ rows).
98	
99	The two frameworks are complementary: RATE measures aggregate quality effects via Likert ATE; CSD targets per-axis style preference via forced-choice comparison, which eliminates ties and is more sensitive to ordinal preferences.
100	The Blindspot arises because Likert tie rates cluster at 58--73\

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	LLM judges are central infrastructure for NLP evaluation (AlpacaEval, MT-Bench, Chatbot Arena) and alignment training (RLHF, DPO), where judge-generated preferences serve as reward signals.
4	Any systematic bias in their assessments propagates through both evaluation leaderboards and model development pipelines.
5	
6	Recent work suggests that style features alone explain most of the variance in judge scores \citep{murugadoss2025sos}, motivating systematic measurement of per-axis style bias.
7	\citet{li2026preference} demonstrate that 76--92\% of observed preference signals originate from surface-level style cues rather than substantive quality differences, and \citet{soumik2026judging} report bias scores of 0.76--0.92 across five different judges.
8	Despite this consensus on severity, existing approaches each address individual requirements: CASSE \citep{casse2026concise} and FairJudge \citep{wang2026fairjudge} provide multi-dimensional style analysis, RATE \citep{reber2025rate} introduces interventional design, and length-controlled AlpacaEval \citep{dubois2025lc} offers population-level correction.
9	However, no single framework integrates all four requirements---per-axis decomposition, interventional identification, per-instance diagnostics, and black-box applicability.
10	A further concern compounds these limitations: standard Likert scoring suffers from a measurement efficiency gap---integer discretization produces ${\sim}60\%$ uninformative ties, and even non-tied pairs carry weaker signal than pairwise comparison---a limitation predicted by measurement theory \citep{thurstone1927law} but previously unquantified for LLM judges.
11	
12	We propose Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a unified framework combining NLI-gated content control, per-axis style decomposition, and position-controlled pairwise comparison to systematically quantify style bias in LLM judges.
13	Inspired by interventional approaches to text analysis \citep{vei
…[truncated 3128 chars]

_source line 3772 · 2026-05-21T22:05:22.528888+00:00_

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Style-Controlled Rewriting}
5	\label{sec:rewriting}
6	
7	We aim to isolate the effect of individual style attributes on judge scores by generating text pairs that differ along exactly one style axis.
8	For each text, an instruction-tuned LLM (Qwen3-32B) first classifies its current style along the target axis (e.g., formal vs.\ casual via binary classification), then rewrites it toward the opposite pole (temperature 0.7) according to an axis-specific prompt (e.g., ``Rewrite the following text to be more formal/casual while preserving the exact same content and meaning'').
9	This produces a dataset balanced across both directions at the dataset level: texts detected as formal are rewritten to casual, and vice versa.
10	Each operation constitutes a binary intervention on a single style variable while all other axes remain fixed (Section~\ref{sec:causal_framework}).

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Model selection.}\nOur target judges span three independent architectures: GPT-4o (2024-08-06), the dominant LLM judge powering AlpacaEval and MT-Bench leaderboards during 2024--2025; Qwen3-32B-Instruct, used as both rewriter and judge for methodological consistency within the Qwen3 generation; and Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct, the latest dense model in the Llama family (Llama~4 adopts a MoE architecture, introducing an additional confound).\nClaude Sonnet~4 serves as negative control.\nWhile newer models have since been released for some families, our framework applies to any black-box judge, and the Blindspot phenomenon is confirmed across three independent architectures spanning both proprietary and open-weight models.\nWe pin model versions for reproducibility; whether the Blindspot persists in successor architectures is an empirical question CSD can readily test.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Model selection.}\nTarget judges span three architectures: GPT-4o (2024-08-06), the dominant judge powering AlpacaEval and MT-Bench leaderboards; Qwen3-32B-Instruct, also serving as rewriter for methodological consistency; and Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct, the latest dense Llama model (Llama~4's MoE architecture introduces an additional confound). Claude Sonnet~4 serves as negative control.\nWe pin versions for reproducibility; CSD applies to any black-box judge, and whether the Blindspot persists in successor architectures is an empirical question it can readily test."
}

171	Register shows judge-dependent heterogeneity: Llama-70B BT 63.5\% ($p = 0.0014$), GPT-4o 57.0\% ($p = 0.04$), Qwen3-32B n.s.\ (55.3\%); all Likert n.s.
172	GEE confirms varying confound structure---Qwen3-32B originality-dominated, GPT-4o and Llama-70B mixed (Table~\ref{tab:gee})---classified as exploratory outside the primary Holm-Bonferroni family.
173	
174	\subsection{GEE Conditional Decomposition and Confound Structure}
175	\label{sec:direction}
176	
177	GEE conditional decomposition \citep{liang1986gee} with exchangeable working correlation and robust standard errors separates style from originality effects (Appendix~\ref{app:direction}; model diagnostics including correlation structure robustness and interaction tests in Appendix~\ref{app:gee_diagnostics}).
178	After controlling for original preference, the formality effect remains significant (GPT-4o: OR $= 2.58$, $p = 0.002$; Qwen3-32B: OR $= 2.15$, $p < 0.0001$, $n = 355$; Llama-70B: OR $= 4.81$, $p < 0.0001$; Table~\ref{tab:gee}); the significant originality ORs confirm residual confounding.
179	The $4\times$ replication ($n = 355$) confirms the Blindspot: BT $67.5\%$ (halved CI), Likert n.s.\ ($p = 0.60$).
180	
181	\begin{table}[t]
182	\centering

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_5/projects/causal_style_decomp_judge/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "Cross-benchmark replication confirms formality bias across all six benchmark$\\times$judge configurations (BT 63--73\\%, all $p < 0.001$); Claude Sonnet 4 shows no detectable bias (BT 56.2\\%/54.1\\%, n.s.; TOST equivalence at $\\Delta = 15$pp, Appendix~\\ref{app:claude_tost}), supporting discriminative validity.\nThe Blindspot manifests in eight of eleven bias-positive cells; three cross-benchmark exceptions (BT ${>}69\\%$, Likert $d \\geq 0.14$) confirm it is effect-size dependent---the same verbosity axis yields a Blindspot on LLMBar (BT 77.3\\%, Likert n.s.) but not on AlpacaEval (BT 84.1\\%, $d = 0.41$), demonstrating a measurement sensitivity boundary rather than a methodological artifact.\nSame-style double-rewrite controls confirm a rewriting artifact (BT $P_{\\text{orig}}$: $.08$--$.42$; Appendix~\\ref{app:artifact_control}), absorbed by GEE's originality term.\nDebiased prompting partially reduces but never eliminates bias across three architectures ($\\Delta = {-}0.4$ to ${-}13.1$pp), with all five debiased estimates remaining significant (all $p < 0.001$), confirming that style preferences are embedded in judge representations.",
  "new_string": "Cross-benchmark replication confirms formality bias across all six benchmark$\\times$judge configurations (BT 63--73\\%, all $p < 0.001$); Claude Sonnet 4 shows no detectable bias (BT 56.2\\%/54.1\\%, n.s.; TOST equivalence at $\\Delta = 15$pp, Appendix~\\ref{app:claude_tost}), supporting discriminative validity.\nThe Blindspot manifests in eight of eleven bias-positive cells; three cross-benchmark exceptions (BT ${>}69\\%$, Likert $d \\geq 0.14$) confirm it is effect-size dependent---the same verbosity axis yields a Blindspot on LLMBar (BT 77.3\\%, Likert n.s.) but not on AlpacaEval (BT 84.1\\%, $d = 0.41$).\nSame-style double-rewrite controls confirm a rewriting artifact (BT $P_{\\text{orig}}$: $.08$--$.42$; Appendix~\\ref{app:artifact_control}), absorbed by GEE's originality term.\nDebiased prompting partially reduces but never eliminates bias ($\\Delta = {-}0.4$ to ${-}13.1$pp, all debiased estimates $p < 0.001$), confirming embedded style preferences."
}

_source line 3800 · 2026-05-21T22:08:00.952661+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a black-box framework yielding two findings across four judges, three benchmarks, and three style axes.
5	First, the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects---complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---confirmed by seven statistical methods.
6	Whether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, Likert-only audits miss style-driven preferences that propagate through alignment pipelines.
7	Second, GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates per-axis confound structure consistent across three architectures---verbosity style-dominated, formality conditional, register judge-dependent---determining which estimates require conditional analysis and informing debiasing priorities.
8	
9	Pairwise comparison offers superior detection sensitivity for style bias, provided that amplification controls---position balancing, content filtering, and per-axis decomposition---are in place (\S\ref{sec:amplification}); existing Likert-based null findings may reflect measurement insensitivity rather than genuine absence of bias.
10	Our findings are established on English benchmarks with $n \leq 355$; validation on larger corpora, additional languages, and emerging judge architectures remains necessary.
11

148	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/power_curve.pdf}
149	\caption{Detection probability at observed effect magnitudes. 80\% power crossings: sign test $n \approx 62$, BT $n \approx 114$, binarized Likert $n \approx 462$, Likert ATE $n \approx 1{,}473$. At typical scales ($n = 50$--$200$, shaded), pairwise methods reach 80\% power while Likert methods remain far below. At $n = 355$, Likert $d = {-}0.025$ confirms the gap is structural, not merely a power issue.}
150	\label{fig:power_curve}
151	\end{figure}
152	
153	\subsection{Cross-Judge Replication}
154	\label{sec:cross_judge}
155	
156	Two independent judges replicate the Blindspot: Qwen3-32B (BT 66.5\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.908$) and Llama-3.3-70B (BT 69.5\%, $p = 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.846$), spanning three architectures (proprietary API, open 32B, open 70B).
157	Qwen3-32B's higher GEE estimate may partly reflect evaluator-producer bias (Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).
158	
159	\subsection{Cross-Axis Replication: Verbosity}
160	\label{sec:cross_axis}
161	
162	The verbosity axis provides the strongest and cleanest demonstration of the Blindspot.
163	All three target judges show consistent verbosity preference: GPT-4o 76.0\% ($p = 0.0001$), Qwen3-32B 77.3\% ($p < 0.0001$), and Llama-70B 73.5\% ($p = 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant for all three ($p = 0.403$, $0.179$, $0.752$).
164	Unlike formality, verbosity is style-dominated across all three judges: GEE decomposition confirms massive style effects (OR $= 8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$) while originality ORs are below 1 ($0.38$--$0.49$)---though Llama-70B's is the only significant one (OR $= 0.38$, $p = .020$)---indicating any artifact favors rewrites over originals rather than reflecting quality confounding (Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
165	Both rewriting directions favor verbose responses (concise$\to$verbose 90.7\%, verbose$\to$concise 80.6\%); controlling for length leaves the style OR unchanged ($<$2\%).
166	Evaluator-produc

69	\label{sec:analysis}

72	\subsection{NLI Gate Validation}
73	\label{sec:nli_validation}
74	
75	\paragraph{False negative rate.}
76	We validate all 100 NLI decisions against an LLM annotator (Qwen3-32B) prompted to judge whether semantic content is preserved.
77	The two systems agree on 91 of 100 pairs (Gwet's AC1 $= 0.89$; Cohen's $\kappa = 0.43$ is depressed by prevalence skew \citep{feinstein1990kappa}).
78	Of the 12 rejected pairs, 8 are judged content-preserving by the LLM annotator, yielding an overall false negative rate of $8/95 = 8.4\%$.
79	Since this annotator shares the same model (Qwen3-32B) as the rewriter, the FNR estimate may be optimistic; the independent GPT-4o cross-validation below provides model-independent verification.
80	Three robustness checks confirm these do not compromise conclusions: BT stable across 11 NLI thresholds from 0.50 to 0.98 (${\leq}3$pp variation across all four judge$\times$axis configurations; \S\ref{sec:nli_threshold}), no covariate differences between retained and discarded pairs (all $p > 0.12$; \S\ref{sec:consistency}), and no directional bias in false negatives (Fisher $p = 0.35$).
81	The NLI gate operates as a precision-oriented conservative filter (precision $= 98.9\%$); verbosity-rejected pairs have larger edit distances (Cliff's $\delta = +0.45$, $p < 0.001$), making estimates lower bounds.

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{False negative rate.}\nWe validate all 100 NLI decisions against an LLM annotator (Qwen3-32B) prompted to judge whether semantic content is preserved.\nThe two systems agree on 91 of 100 pairs (Gwet's AC1 $= 0.89$; Cohen's $\\kappa = 0.43$ is depressed by prevalence skew \\citep{feinstein1990kappa}).\nOf the 12 rejected pairs, 8 are judged content-preserving by the LLM annotator, yielding an overall false negative rate of $8/95 = 8.4\\%$.\nSince this annotator shares the same model (Qwen3-32B) as the rewriter, the FNR estimate may be optimistic; the independent GPT-4o cross-validation below provides model-independent verification.\nThree robustness checks confirm these do not compromise conclusions: BT stable across 11 NLI thresholds from 0.50 to 0.98 (${\\leq}3$pp variation across all four judge$\\times$axis configurations; \\S\\ref{sec:nli_threshold}), no covariate differences between retained and discarded pairs (all $p > 0.12$; \\S\\ref{sec:consistency}), and no directional bias in false negatives (Fisher $p = 0.35$).\nThe NLI gate operates as a precision-oriented conservative filter (precision $= 98.9\\%$); verbosity-rejected pairs have larger edit distances (Cliff's $\\delta = +0.45$, $p < 0.001$), making estimates lower bounds.\nFull-scale GPT-4o validation on all 200 NLI gate decisions yields 92.1\\% pass precision for formality and 82.6\\% for verbosity, with the lower verbosity figure reflecting the permissiveness of direction-aware gating (17.4\\% false positives vs.\\ 7.9\\% for the strict bidirectional formality gate---a trade-off for the higher pass rate that enables adequate sample sizes).\nAdmitted false positives would inflate verbosity BT only if content-leaked pairs systematically favor the styled variant; the GEE originality OR ${<}1$ indicates the opposite direction, making the verbosity BT estimate a conservative lower bound on pure style preference.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.}\n\\label{sec:nli_threshold}\nA continuous sweep across 11 NLI thresholds (0.50--0.98) confirms that BT estimates are robust to threshold choice: all 44 judge$\\times$axis$\\times$threshold combinations are significant, with maximum variation ${\\leq}3$pp within each configuration (Appendix~\\ref{app:nli_sweep}).\nNLI scores follow a bimodal distribution (most pairs above 0.95 or below 0.30), so the 0.90 threshold lies on a plateau rather than a cliff edge.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{False negative rate.}\nValidating all 100 NLI decisions against an LLM annotator (Qwen3-32B) yields 91/100 agreement (Gwet's AC1 $= 0.89$; Cohen's $\\kappa = 0.43$, depressed by prevalence skew \\citep{feinstein1990kappa}), with FNR $= 8.4\\%$ (8 of 12 rejected pairs judged content-preserving).\nSince this annotator shares the rewriter model, the FNR may be optimistic; GPT-4o cross-validation below provides model-independent verification.\nThree robustness checks confirm these do not compromise conclusions: BT stable across 11 NLI thresholds (${\\leq}3$pp variation; \\S\\ref{sec:nli_threshold}), no covariate differences between retained and discarded pairs ($p > 0.12$; \\S\\ref{sec:consistency}), and no directional bias in false negatives (Fisher $p = 0.35$).\nThe gate operates as a precision-oriented filter (precision $= 98.9\\%$); verbosity-rejected pairs have larger edit distances (Cliff's $\\delta = +0.45$, $p < 0.001$), making estimates lower bounds.\nFull-scale GPT-4o validation on all 200 decisions yields 92.1\\% formality / 82.6\\% verbosity pass precision, the lower verbosity figure reflecting direction-aware gating permissiveness (17.4\\% vs.\\ 7.9\\% FPR).\nAdmitted false positives would inflate verbosity BT only if content-leaked pairs systematically favor the styled variant; the GEE originality OR ${<}1$ indicates the opposite direction, making the estimate a conservative lower bound.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.}\n\\label{sec:nli_threshold}\nA sweep across 11 NLI thresholds (0.50--0.98) confirms robustness: all 44 judge$\\times$axis$\\times$threshold combinations are significant (${\\leq}3$pp variation; Appendix~\\ref{app:nli_sweep}), and the bimodal NLI score distribution places the 0.90 threshold on a plateau."

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_5/projects/causal_style_decomp_judge/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "Bootstrap subsampling across seven sample sizes confirms this pattern: $d$ remains flat near zero ($d_{\\text{median}} = {-}0.017$) as CIs narrow from ${\\pm}0.3$ to ${\\pm}0.1$, never excluding zero (Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_attenuation}).\nAttenuation follows a severity spectrum.\nAt moderate bias (BT 63--67\\%), it is complete: $d$ converges to ${\\approx}0$ even at $n = 355$, rendering the bias invisible to any Likert-based test.\nAt stronger effects (BT ${>}70\\%$), Likert partially detects but severely attenuates: MT-Bench formality yields Likert $d \\approx 0.15$ for BT ${\\sim}70\\%$ effects---${>}60\\%$ signal compression---explaining why three of eleven bias-positive cells escape the Blindspot (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}, $\\ddagger$ rows).\n\nThe two frameworks are complementary: RATE measures aggregate quality effects via Likert ATE; CSD targets per-axis style preference via forced-choice comparison, which eliminates ties and is more sensitive to ordinal preferences.\nThe Blindspot arises because Likert tie rates cluster at 58--73\\% across all nine judge--axis configurations.\nA 100-point continuous scale ($0$--$100$) does not resolve this: formality remains non-significant ($p = 0.725$, tie rate $65.9\\%$) and verbosity likewise ($p = 0.117$, tie rate $51.2\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:likert100}), demonstrating that the Blindspot reflects a paradigm difference between absolute and relative evaluation rather than scale granularity.",
  "new_string": "Bootstrap subsampling confirms: $d$ remains flat near zero ($d_{\\text{median}} = {-}0.017$) as CIs narrow from ${\\pm}0.3$ to ${\\pm}0.1$, never excluding zero (Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_attenuation}).\nAttenuation follows a severity spectrum: at moderate bias (BT 63--67\\%), it is complete ($d \\to 0$ at $n = 355$); at stronger effects (BT ${>}70\\%$), Likert partially detects but attenuates ${>}60\\%$ (MT-Bench $d \\approx 0.15$ for BT ${\\sim}70\\%$)---explaining why three of eleven bias-positive cells escape the Blindspot (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}, $\\ddagger$ rows).\n\nThe frameworks are complementary: RATE measures aggregate quality via Likert ATE; CSD targets per-axis style preference via forced-choice comparison, eliminating ties.\nThe Blindspot arises because Likert tie rates cluster at 58--73\\% across all nine configurations; a 100-point continuous scale does not resolve this ($p = 0.725$/$0.117$; Appendix~\\ref{app:likert100}), confirming a paradigm difference rather than granularity limitation."
}

_source line 3832 · 2026-05-21T22:25:46.493362+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM judges are widely deployed for evaluation and alignment, yet style features alone explain judge scores with near-perfect correlation.
3	We introduce Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a unified framework combining NLI-gated content control, per-axis style decomposition, and position-controlled pairwise comparison to systematically quantify style bias in LLM judges.
4	Across three target judges, one negative control, three benchmarks, and three style axes, CSD demonstrates the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects, following a severity spectrum from complete attenuation at moderate bias to over 60\% signal loss at stronger effects, confirmed by seven statistical methods.
5	GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates a per-axis confound structure consistent across all three target judges: verbosity bias is style-dominated, formality exhibits a conditional effect moderated by content quality, and register is judge-dependent---determining which estimates are directly interpretable and which require conditional analysis.
6	A negative-control judge shows no detectable bias on either primary axis, supporting discriminative validity.
7	Whether this divergence reflects measurement attenuation or genuine construct divergence, both interpretations yield the same operational conclusion: Likert-only evaluation systematically misses style-driven preferences that propagate through pairwise-based alignment pipelines.
8	\end{abstract}
9

2	\label{sec:experiments}

5	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
6	\label{sec:stat_framework}
7	
8	We designate a primary family of six hypothesis tests (three judges $\times$ two axes: formality and verbosity) and control the family-wise error rate with Holm-Bonferroni correction at $\alpha = 0.05$.
9	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports both raw and adjusted $p$-values.
10	Analyses outside this grid are classified as either \emph{exploratory} (NLI threshold sensitivity sweep, \S\ref{sec:nli_threshold}) or \emph{confirmatory} (consistency filter validation, \S\ref{sec:consistency}).
11	Exploratory analyses are reported with uncorrected $p$-values and interpreted as hypothesis-generating; confirmatory analyses test specific hypotheses about pipeline validity and are evaluated at the nominal $\alpha = 0.05$ level without additional correction, as they address orthogonal questions about measurement reliability rather than the primary style-bias family.
12	Bradley-Terry parameters are estimated via spectral initialization followed by minorization-maximization \citep{hunter2004mm}, using the default regularization of the \texttt{choix} Python library.
13	All bootstrap confidence intervals use 10{,}000 pair-level resamples with seed 42.
14	
15	\paragraph{Model selection.}
16	Target judges span three architectures: GPT-4o (2024-08-06), the dominant judge powering AlpacaEval and MT-Bench leaderboards; Qwen3-32B-Instruct, also serving as rewriter for methodological consistency; and Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct, the latest dense Llama model (Llama~4's MoE architecture introduces an additional confound). Claude Sonnet~4 serves as negative control.
17	We pin versions for reproducibility; CSD applies to any black-box judge, and whether the Blindspot persists in successor architectures is an empirical question it can readily test.

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	LLM judges are central infrastructure for NLP evaluation (AlpacaEval, MT-Bench, Chatbot Arena) and alignment training (RLHF, DPO), where judge-generated preferences serve as reward signals.
4	Any systematic bias in their assessments propagates through both evaluation leaderboards and model development pipelines.
5	
6	Recent work suggests that style features alone explain most of the variance in judge scores \citep{murugadoss2025sos}, motivating systematic measurement of per-axis style bias.
7	\citet{li2026preference} demonstrate that 76--92\% of observed preference signals originate from surface-level style cues rather than substantive quality differences, and \citet{soumik2026judging} report bias scores of 0.76--0.92 across five different judges.
8	Despite this consensus on severity, existing approaches each address individual requirements: CASSE \citep{casse2026concise} and FairJudge \citep{wang2026fairjudge} provide multi-dimensional style analysis, RATE \citep{reber2025rate} introduces interventional design, and length-controlled AlpacaEval \citep{dubois2025lc} offers population-level correction.
9	However, no single framework integrates all four requirements---per-axis decomposition, interventional identification, per-instance diagnostics, and black-box applicability.
10	A further concern compounds these limitations: standard Likert scoring suffers from a measurement efficiency gap---integer discretization produces ${\sim}60\%$ uninformative ties, and even non-tied pairs carry weaker signal than pairwise comparison---a limitation predicted by measurement theory \citep{thurstone1927law} but previously unquantified for LLM judges.
11	
12	We propose Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a unified framework combining NLI-gated content control, per-axis style decomposition, and position-controlled pairwise comparison to systematically quantify style bias in LLM judges.

_source line 3838 · 2026-05-21T22:25:47.901079+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a black-box framework yielding two findings across four judges, three benchmarks, and three style axes.
5	First, the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects---complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---confirmed by seven statistical methods.
6	Whether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, Likert-only audits miss style-driven preferences that propagate through alignment pipelines.
7	Second, GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates per-axis confound structure consistent across three architectures---verbosity style-dominated, formality conditional, register judge-dependent---determining which estimates require conditional analysis and informing debiasing priorities.
8	
9	Pairwise comparison offers superior detection sensitivity for style bias, provided that amplification controls are in place (\S\ref{sec:amplification}); existing Likert-based null findings may reflect measurement insensitivity rather than genuine absence of bias.
10	Our findings are established on English benchmarks with $n \leq 355$; validation on larger corpora, additional languages, and emerging architectures remains necessary.
11

145	\end{figure}
146	
147	\subsection{Cross-Judge Replication}
148	\label{sec:cross_judge}
149	
150	Two independent judges replicate the Blindspot: Qwen3-32B (BT 66.5\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.908$) and Llama-3.3-70B (BT 69.5\%, $p = 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.846$), spanning three architectures (proprietary API, open 32B, open 70B).
151	Qwen3-32B's higher GEE estimate may partly reflect evaluator-producer bias (Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).
152	
153	\subsection{Cross-Axis Replication: Verbosity}
154	\label{sec:cross_axis}
155	
156	Verbosity provides the strongest Blindspot: all three judges show consistent preference (GPT-4o 76.0\%, Qwen3-32B 77.3\%, Llama-70B 73.5\%; all $p \leq 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant ($p = 0.403$, $0.179$, $0.752$).
157	Unlike formality, verbosity is style-dominated: GEE confirms massive style effects (OR $8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$) with originality ORs below 1 ($0.38$--$0.49$; only Llama-70B significant at $p = .020$), indicating artifacts favor rewrites rather than reflecting quality confounding (Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
158	Both rewriting directions favor verbose responses (90.7\%/80.6\%); controlling for length leaves the style OR unchanged ($<$2\%).
159	Evaluator-producer overlap applies only to Qwen3-32B (both rewriter and judge); an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds this to a $22\%$ Style OR reduction ($13.10 \to 10.17$; Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}). GPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B use a separate rewriter and are not subject to self-preference bias.
160	
161	\subsection{Register: Judge-Dependent Confound Structure}
162	\label{sec:negative_control}
163	
164	Register shows judge-dependent heterogeneity: Llama-70B BT 63.5\% ($p = 0.0014$), GPT-4o 57.0\% ($p = 0.04$), Qwen3-32B n.s.\ (55.3\%); all Likert n.s.
165	GEE confirms varying confound structure---Qwen3-32B originality-dominated, GPT-4o and Llama-70B mixed (Table~\ref{tab:gee})---classified as exploratory outside the primary Holm-Bonferroni family.

38	\section*{Limitations}
39	\label{sec:limitations}
40	
41	\paragraph{Quality--style confounding and construct validity.}
42	The NLI gate blocks content changes but cannot block quality perception shifts mediated by style itself---a fundamental identifiability limit of text-rewriting approaches.
43	Although the formality effect survives logistic adjustment for original preference ($\text{OR} = 2.32$, $p = 0.006$; Appendix~\ref{app:direction}), residual confounding is not fully eliminated.
44	An alternative interpretation of the Blindspot is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs: Likert captures absolute quality difference while pairwise captures ordinal preference. Under this view, the divergence reflects construct difference rather than measurement failure. The Comparative Trap \citep{jeong2025comparative} lends support to this alternative; six experimental controls bound this risk (\S\ref{sec:amplification}), though definitive separation would require human ground-truth ratings.
45	
46	\paragraph{Evaluator-producer bias and generalizability.}
47	Qwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge, raising self-preference concerns \citep{panickssery2024selfpreference}; an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds this to a 22\% Style OR reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).
48	GPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B use a separate rewriter and are not subject to evaluator-producer overlap; both independently confirm the Blindspot across three architectures, though cross-judge effect-size comparisons are not valid.
49	Register results carry the highest evaluator-producer risk---all register rewrites use the same Qwen3-32B rewriter without the cross-rewriter validation applied to formality and verbosity---and register conclusions remain tentative pending independent rewriter validation.
50	All experiments use LLMBar ($n = 100$); additional axes, larger benchmarks, and further judges (e.g., Gemini) are needed.
51	Our per-axis decomposition treats each style axis ind

_source line 3864 · 2026-05-21T22:28:46.248104+00:00_

25	\subsection{Measurement Protocol}
26	\label{sec:measurement}
27	
28	Each pair that passes the NLI gate is evaluated by three target judges---GPT-4o (\texttt{gpt-4o-2024-08-06}), Qwen3-32B, and Llama-3.3-70B---under two complementary scoring modes.
29	In Likert mode, the judge assigns an integer score from 1 to 10 for overall quality to each text independently.
30	In pairwise mode, the judge receives both texts as candidates A and B and makes a forced choice of which response is better.
31	
32	To control for position bias, each pairwise comparison is evaluated twice with the presentation order swapped, which produces $n_{\text{pairs}} \times 2$ observations.
33	We apply two analysis strategies to these data.
34	For the nonparametric sign test, we retain only \emph{consistent} pairs where the judge selected the same text in both orderings.

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_5/projects/causal_style_decomp_judge/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "Two independent judges replicate the Blindspot: Qwen3-32B (BT 66.5\\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.908$) and Llama-3.3-70B (BT 69.5\\%, $p = 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.846$), spanning three architectures (proprietary API, open 32B, open 70B).\nQwen3-32B's higher GEE estimate may partly reflect evaluator-producer bias (Appendix~\\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).",
  "new_string": "Three independent judges replicate the Blindspot: Qwen3-32B (BT 66.5\\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.908$), Llama-3.3-70B (BT 69.5\\%, $p = 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.846$), and Gemini-2.5-Flash (confirmatory: BT 70.8\\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $d = 0.05$, n.s.), spanning four architectures.\nFor formality, Gemini shows a weaker, primarily originality-driven preference (BT 57.4\\%, $p = 0.045$; GEE Style OR $= 1.64$, n.s.; Orig OR $= 2.51$, $p = 0.002$), extending the confound taxonomy: while formality is conditionally style-driven in GPT-4o, Qwen3, and Llama, it is originality-dominated in Gemini---further evidence that confound structure is both axis- and judge-dependent."
}
```

</details>

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_5/projects/causal_style_decomp_judge/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "Verbosity provides the strongest Blindspot: all three judges show consistent preference (GPT-4o 76.0\\%, Qwen3-32B 77.3\\%, Llama-70B 73.5\\%; all $p \\leq 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant ($p = 0.403$, $0.179$, $0.752$).\nUnlike formality, verbosity is style-dominated: GEE confirms massive style effects (OR $8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$) with originality ORs below 1 ($0.38$--$0.49$; only Llama-70B significant at $p = .020$), indicating artifacts favor rewrites rather than reflecting quality confounding (Table~\\ref{tab:gee}).",
  "new_string": "Verbosity provides the strongest Blindspot: all four target judges show consistent preference (GPT-4o 76.0\\%, Qwen3-32B 77.3\\%, Llama-70B 73.5\\%, Gemini 70.8\\%; all $p \\leq 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant for all four ($p = 0.403$, $0.179$, $0.752$, $0.370$).\nVerbosity is style-dominated across all four judges: GEE confirms massive style effects (OR $8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$) with originality ORs below 1 ($0.38$--$0.49$; only Llama-70B significant at $p = .020$), indicating artifacts favor rewrites rather than reflecting quality confounding (Table~\\ref{tab:gee})."
}
```

_source line 3890 · 2026-05-21T22:30:39.030562+00:00_

134	An equally plausible interpretation is that Likert and pairwise modes measure different constructs---absolute quality vs.\ ordinal preference---making the divergence a construct difference rather than measurement failure.
135	\citet{tripathi2025pairwise} independently confirm that pairwise and pointwise protocols exhibit distinct sensitivity profiles, with pairwise preferences being more volatile---consistent with this construct divergence interpretation.
136	Both interpretations lead to the same conclusion: Likert audits miss style preferences that drive pairwise-based alignment pipelines (RLHF, DPO).
137	
138	\paragraph{Detection vs.\ amplification.}
139	\label{sec:amplification}
140	\citet{jeong2025comparative} show that pairwise comparison amplifies style biases (the Comparative Trap).
141	While format-level amplification of micro-preferences cannot be fully excluded, six independent controls bound this risk: (i)~NLI gate ablation reverses NLI-fail pairs to BT 41.7\% (Appendix~\ref{app:nli_ablation}); (ii)~Claude Sonnet 4 shows no bias with the identical pairwise format (BT 56.2\%/54.1\%, n.s.); (iii)~cross-axis BT variation (register 55\% $<$ formality 67\% $<$ verbosity 77\%) is inconsistent with uniform format amplification; (iv)~rewriting artifact absorbed by GEE decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:artifact_control}); (v)~debiased prompting partially reduces bias (verbosity $-13.1$pp; formality $-5.7$/$-0.4$pp); (vi)~three independent architectures all confirm the Blindspot with consistent GEE confound structure (\S\ref{sec:cross_judge}).
142	
143	\begin{figure}[t]

_source line 3912 · 2026-05-21T22:32:23.591494+00:00_

199	GPT-4o reg.\ & 1.88 & .029 & 2.21 & .006 \\
200	Qwen3 reg.\ & 1.91 & .046 & 5.72 & ${<}.0001$ \\
201	Llama-70B reg.\ & 3.33 & .0003 & 3.07 & .0008 \\
202	\bottomrule
203	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\footnotesize $^a$ $n = 355$; other rows: $n = 88$. 95\% CIs in Appendix.}
204	\end{tabular}
205	\end{table}
206	
207	The confound structure is consistent across all four target judges for verbosity (style-dominated: Style OR $8.2$--$13.1$, Orig OR $0.38$--$0.49$); formality is conditionally style-driven in three judges but originality-dominated in Gemini (Style OR $1.64$, n.s.); register is judge-dependent.
208	Cross-benchmark replication confirms formality bias across all six benchmark$\times$judge configurations (BT 63--73\%, all $p < 0.001$); Claude Sonnet 4 shows no detectable bias (BT 56.2\%/54.1\%, n.s.; TOST equivalence at $\Delta = 15$pp, Appendix~\ref{app:claude_tost}), supporting discriminative validity.
209	The Blindspot manifests in eight of eleven bias-positive cells; three cross-benchmark exceptions (BT ${>}69\%$, Likert $d \geq 0.14$) confirm it is effect-size dependent---the same verbosity axis yields a Blindspot on LLMBar (BT 77.3\%, Likert n.s.) but not on AlpacaEval (BT 84.1\%, $d = 0.41$).
210	Same-style double-rewrite controls confirm a rewriting artifact (BT $P_{\text{orig}}$: $.08$--$.42$; Appendix~\ref{app:artifact_control}), absorbed by GEE's originality term.
211	Debiased prompting partially reduces but never eliminates bias ($\Delta = {-}0.4$ to ${-}13.1$pp, all debiased estimates $p < 0.001$), confirming embedded style preferences.
212

_source line 3936 · 2026-05-21T22:36:49.221878+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Patch] Figure 1 "Blindspot in Action" — 使用 mvp_0048

**选中候选**: mvp_0048 "average height for a male"

**数据**：

Instruction: *"What is the average height for a male?"*

**Original (formal):**
> Yes, men are taller than women on average. The worldwide average height for men is 175 cm (69 inches, or 5 feet 9 inches), while the worldwide average height for women is 162 cm (64 inches, or 5 feet 4 inches).

**Casual rewrite:**
> Sure, men are generally taller than women. On average, guys around the world are about 175 cm tall—roughly 5 feet 9 inches—which is taller than the average woman's height of 162 cm, or about 5 feet 4 inches.

NLI: 0.998 (content preserved ✓)

| Judge | Likert (formal / casual) | Pairwise |
|-------|--------------------------|----------|
| GPT-4o | 5 / **8** (casual +3) | **formal wins** (both orderings) |
| Llama-70B | 8 / 8 (tie) | **formal wins** (both orderings) |
| Qwen3-32B | 7 / 6 (formal +1) | **formal wins** (both orderings) |

**为什么选这个**: GPT-4o 的矛盾最戏剧性——Likert 给 casual 打 8（比 formal 的 5 高 3 分），却在 pairwise 中选 formal。同一个 judge、同一对文本、相反结论。**这就是 Blindspot 的完美缩影。**

**放置方式**: 方案 B（§1 Introduction 开头 motivating example）。

建议排版：一个 figure/box 展示：
1. 上方：Instruction + 两个版本的文本（左 formal / 右 casual），标注 NLI=0.998
2. 下方：一个小表格或标注显示 GPT-4o 的 Likert vs Pairwise 矛盾
3. Caption: "The Likert Blindspot in action. GPT-4o rates the casual variant higher (8 vs 5) in absolute scoring, yet consistently prefers the formal variant in pairwise comparison. Across [N] such pairs, this pattern averages to Likert ATE ≈ 0 (n.s.) while pairwise BT detects significant formal preference (63%, p < 0.001). Content equivalence is verified by NLI (0.998)."

**页面预算**: 这会占 ~1/3 页。需要在 Intro 中压缩等量空间（现有 Intro 可能有冗余的 gap enumeration 可以精简）。如果实在不够，可以把 figure 做得更紧凑（去掉完整文本，只展示关键片段）。

现有 pipeline diagram 改编号为 Figure 2。

完成后编译确认 ≤8 页。

_source line 3938 · 2026-05-21T22:37:01.694164+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	LLM judges are central infrastructure for NLP evaluation (AlpacaEval, MT-Bench, Chatbot Arena) and alignment training (RLHF, DPO), where judge-generated preferences serve as reward signals.
4	Any systematic bias in their assessments propagates through both evaluation leaderboards and model development pipelines.
5	
6	Recent work suggests that style features alone explain most of the variance in judge scores \citep{murugadoss2025sos}, motivating systematic measurement of per-axis style bias.
7	\citet{li2026preference} demonstrate that 76--92\% of observed preference signals originate from surface-level style cues rather than substantive quality differences, and \citet{soumik2026judging} report bias scores of 0.76--0.92 across five different judges.
8	Despite this consensus on severity, existing approaches each address individual requirements: CASSE \citep{casse2026concise} and FairJudge \citep{wang2026fairjudge} provide multi-dimensional style analysis, RATE \citep{reber2025rate} introduces interventional design, and length-controlled AlpacaEval \citep{dubois2025lc} offers population-level correction.
9	However, no single framework integrates all four requirements---per-axis decomposition, interventional identification, per-instance diagnostics, and black-box applicability.
10	A further concern compounds these limitations: standard Likert scoring suffers from a measurement efficiency gap---integer discretization produces ${\sim}60\%$ uninformative ties, and even non-tied pairs carry weaker signal than pairwise comparison---a limitation predicted by measurement theory \citep{thurstone1927law} but previously unquantified for LLM judges.
11	
12	We propose Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a unified framework combining NLI-gated content control, per-axis style decomposition, and position-controlled pairwise comparison to systematically quantify style bias in LLM judges.
13	Inspired by interventional approaches to text analysis \citep{vei

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	
5	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
6	\label{sec:stat_framework}
7	
8	We designate a primary family of six hypothesis tests (three judges $\times$ two axes: formality and verbosity) and control the family-wise error rate with Holm-Bonferroni correction at $\alpha = 0.05$.
9	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports both raw and adjusted $p$-values.
10	Analyses outside this grid are classified as either \emph{exploratory} (NLI threshold sensitivity sweep, \S\ref{sec:nli_threshold}) or \emph{confirmatory} (consistency filter validation, \S\ref{sec:consistency}).
11	Exploratory analyses are reported with uncorrected $p$-values and interpreted as hypothesis-generating; confirmatory analyses test specific hypotheses about pipeline validity and are evaluated at the nominal $\alpha = 0.05$ level without additional correction, as they address orthogonal questions about measurement reliability rather than the primary style-bias family.
12	Bradley-Terry parameters are estimated via spectral initialization followed by minorization-maximization \citep{hunter2004mm}, using the default regularization of the \texttt{choix} Python library.
13	All bootstrap confidence intervals use 10{,}000 pair-level resamples with seed 42.
14	
15	\paragraph{Model selection.}
16	Target judges span four architectures: GPT-4o (2024-08-06), the dominant judge powering AlpacaEval and MT-Bench leaderboards; Qwen3-32B-Instruct, also serving as rewriter for methodological consistency; Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct, the latest dense Llama model (Llama~4's MoE architecture introduces an additional confound); and Gemini-2.5-Flash as a confirmatory fourth architecture. Claude Sonnet~4 serves as negative control.
17	We pin versions for reproducibility; CSD applies to any black-box judge.
18

  "old_string": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLLM judges are central infrastructure for NLP evaluation (AlpacaEval, MT-Bench, Chatbot Arena) and alignment training (RLHF, DPO), where judge-generated preferences serve as reward signals.\nAny systematic bias in their assessments propagates through both evaluation leaderboards and model development pipelines.\n\nRecent work suggests that style features alone explain most of the variance in judge scores \\citep{murugadoss2025sos}, motivating systematic measurement of per-axis style bias.\n\\citet{li2026preference} demonstrate that 76--92\\% of observed preference signals originate from surface-level style cues rather than substantive quality differences, and \\citet{soumik2026judging} report bias scores of 0.76--0.92 across five different judges.\nDespite this consensus on severity, existing approaches each address individual requirements: CASSE \\citep{casse2026concise} and FairJudge \\citep{wang2026fairjudge} provide multi-dimensional style analysis, RATE \\citep{reber2025rate} introduces interventional design, and length-controlled AlpacaEval \\citep{dubois2025lc} offers population-level correction.\nHowever, no single framework integrates all four requirements---per-axis decomposition, interventional identification, per-instance diagnostics, and black-box applicability.\nA further concern compounds these limitations: standard Likert scoring suffers from a measurement efficiency gap---integer discretization produces ${\\sim}60\\%$ uninformative ties, and even non-tied pairs carry weaker signal than pairwise comparison---a limitation predicted by measurement theory \\citep{thurstone1927law} but previously unquantified for LLM judges.\n\nWe propose Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a unified framework combining NLI-gated content control, per-axis style decomposition, and position-controlled pairwise comparison to systematically quantify style bias in LLM judges.\nInspired by interventional approaches to text analysis \\citep{veitch2020adapting}, CSD operates in three stages: binary style-controlled rewriting along a single axis (e.g., formal vs.\\ casual), content preservation verification through Natural Language Inference (NLI) entailment scoring, and bias measurement via position-controlled pairwise comparison.\nBecause CSD requires no access to judge internals and applies post-hoc to any black-box judge, it complements existing population-level approaches with finer-grained, per-instance diagnostics.\nOur experiments quantify this efficiency gap---which we term the \\emph{Likert Blindspot}---across four target judges (GPT-4o, Qwen3-32B, Llama-3.3-70B, Gemini-2.5-Flash) and one negative control (Claude Sonnet 4), three benchmarks, and two primary style axes (formality, verbosity), with exploratory evidence on register.\nOn the same formality data, Likert scoring yields non-significant ATE ($p = 0.332$, $p = 0.908$) for both judges, while Bradley-Terry analysis detects 63.0\\% (GPT-4o, $p = 0.0004$) and 66.5\\% (Qwen3-32B, $p < 0.0001$) formal win probabilities; verbosity shows 77.3\\% BT ($p < 0.0001$) invisible to Likert ($p = 0.179$; Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{A black-box controlled framework} for detecting per-axis style bias in LLM judges through style-controlled rewriting and position-controlled pairwise comparison (Section~\\ref{sec:method}).\n    \\item \\textbf{The Likert Blindspot:} Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects, following a severity spectrum---complete at moderate bias ($d \\to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---across six judge$\\times$axis configurations ($p < 0.001$ pairwise vs.\\ $p > 0.17$ Likert). Whether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, the operational consequence is identical; seven statistical methods confirm the effect is structural (Section~\\ref{sec:analysis}, Tables~\\ref{tab:main_results}--\\ref{tab:sensitivity}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Per-axis confound structure:} GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates that verbosity bias is style-dominated across all four target judges (Style OR $8$--$13$, Orig OR ${<}1$), formality is conditional (significant style and originality effects), and register is judge-dependent (originality-dominated to mixed)---this structure determines which CSD estimates are directly interpretable (verbosity) versus requiring conditional interpretation (formality), and is invisible to aggregate metrics (Section~\\ref{sec:direction}, Table~\\ref{tab:gee}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Practical implications:} style bias audits require pairwise comparison \\emph{and} per-axis decomposition; aggregate metrics and Likert-based findings may systematically underestimate bias or conflate distinct confound structures (Section~\\ref{sec:conclusion}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_diagram.pdf}\n\\caption{CSD pipeline overview. Numbers indicate sample attrition at each stage.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}",

preferences serve as reward signals; any systematic bias propagates through both evaluation leaderboards and model development pipelines.\nStyle features alone explain most of the variance in judge scores \\citep{murugadoss2025sos}: 76--92\\% of observed preferences originate from surface-level style cues \\citep{li2026preference}, with bias scores of 0.76--0.92 across five judges \\citep{soumik2026judging}.\nDespite this consensus, existing approaches each address individual requirements---multi-dimensional style analysis \\citep{casse2026concise,wang2026fairjudge}, interventional design \\citep{reber2025rate}, population-level correction \\citep{dubois2025lc}---but no single framework integrates per-axis decomposition, interventional identification, per-instance comparison---a limitation predicted by measurement theory \\citep{thurstone1927law} but previously unquantified for LLM judges (Figure~\\ref{fig:blindspot_example}).\n\nWe propose Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), combining NLI-gated content control, per-axis style decomposition, and position-controlled pairwise comparison.\nInspired by interventional text analysis \\citep{veitch2020adapting}, CSD operates in three stages: style-controlled rewriting along a single axis, NLI entailment verification, and bias measurement via forced-choice comparison.\nCSD requires no access to judge internals and applies post-hoc to any black-box judge.\nOur experiments reveal the \\emph{Likert Blindspot} across four target judges (GPT-4o, Qwen3-32B, Llama-3.3-70B, Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{A black-box controlled framework} for per-axis style bias detection via style-controlled rewriting and position-controlled pairwise comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:method}).\n    \\item \\textbf{The Likert Blindspot:} Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects---complete at moderate bias ($d \\to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---across six judge$\\times$axis configurations ($p < 0.001$ pairwise vs.\\ $p > 0.17$ Likert), confirmed by seven statistical methods (Tables~\\ref{tab:main_results}--\\ref{tab:sensitivity}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Per-axis confound structure:} GEE decomposition shows verbosity is style-dominated (Style OR $8$--$13$, Orig OR ${<}1$), formality conditional, register judge-dependent---determining which estimates require conditional analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:gee}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Practical implications:} audits require pairwise comparison \\emph{and} per-axis decomposition; Likert-based findings may systematically underestimate bias no access to judge internals and applies post-hoc to any black-box judge.\nOur experiments reveal the \\emph{Likert Blindspot} across four target judges (GPT-4o, Qwen3-32B, Llama-3.3-70B, Gemini-2.5-Flash), one negative control (Claude Sonnet 4), three benchmarks, and two primary style axes, with exploratory register evidence.\nOn formality data, Likert yields non-significant ATE ($p = 0.332$, $0.908$), while BT detects 63.0\\% (GPT-4o, $p = 0.0004$) and 66.5\\% (Qwen3-32B, $p < 0.0001$) formal preferences; verbosity shows 77.3\\% BT ($p < 0.0001$) invisible to Likert ($p = 0.179$; Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{A black-box controlled framework} for per-axis style bias detection via style-controlled rewriting and position-controlled pairwise comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:method}).\n    \\item \\textbf{The Likert Blindspot:} Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects---complete at moderate bias ($d \\to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---across six judge$\\times$axis configurations ($p < 0.001$ pairwise vs.\\ $p > 0.17$ Likert), confirmed by seven statistical methods (Tables~\\ref{tab:main_results}--\\ref{tab:sensitivity}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Per-axis confound structure:} GEE decomposition shows verbosity is style-dominated (Style OR $8$--$13$, Orig OR ${<}1$), formality conditional, register judge-dependent---determining which estimates require conditional analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:gee}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Practical implications:} audits require pairwise comparison \\emph{and} per-axis decomposition; Likert-based findings may systematically underestimate bias (\\S\\ref{sec:conclusion}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_diagram.pdf}\n\\caption{CSD pipeline overview. Numbers indicate sample attrition at each stage.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}"
  }
}
```

[Director 指令] [Patch] 两个任务，按优先级排序：

**任务 1：页面压缩（最高优先级）**
当前 main text（Abstract 到 Conclusion 结束）溢出约 0.2 页——Conclusion 文本流到了第 9 页顶部才结束，然后才是 Limitations。EMNLP 2026 严格 8 页限制（main text，不含 Limitations/references/appendix）。

压缩策略：
- §5.5 Register 段落可以精简（register 本身是 exploratory，不需要长篇描述）
- §5.3 Cross-Judge Replication 段落现在有 Gemini 内容，检查是否有冗余
- §5.6 GEE Conditional Decomposition 可以考虑精简重复的跨 judge 描述
- Conclusion 本身也可以更紧凑
- 目标：main text 严格 ≤ 8 页，留出 ~3 行的 margin

**任务 2：Figure 1 "Blindspot in Action" Case Study**
在 Introduction 开头添加一个文字 case study 作为 motivating example（不是图表，是文字 example），替代数字统计作为 hook。用 mvp_0048 数据：

- Instruction: "average height for a male"
- Original (formal): "Yes, men are taller than women on average. The worldwide average height for men is 175 cm (69 inches, or 5 feet 9 inches)..."
- Casual rewrite: "Sure, men are generally taller than women. On average, guys around the world are about 175 cm tall..."
- NLI entailment: 0.993 (content preserved)
- GPT-4o Likert: casual=8, formal=5 — casual rated HIGHER
- GPT-4o Pairwise: formal wins (both orderings consistent)
- 即 GPT-4o 在 Likert 下给 casual 更高分，但 pairwise 下选择 formal — 这就是 Blindspot

写法：3-4 句话的 vignette，放在 Introduction 第一段之前或之内，用 italic 或 small example 格式。重点是 "aha moment" — reviewer 前 30 秒就直观理解 Blindspot 是什么。

注意：添加 case study 后 main text 仍须 ≤ 8 页。如果空间不够，优先保证任务 1 的页面压缩。Case study 可以很短（2-3 句也行）。

这次不用验证引用。编译后确认 main text ≤ 8 页。

18	Casual & \textbf{8} & loses \\

22	\caption{The Likert Blindspot in action. GPT-4o rates the casual variant higher (8 vs.\ 5) in absolute scoring, yet consistently prefers the formal variant in pairwise comparison---same judge, same texts, opposite conclusions. Across $n = 88$ such pairs, this pattern averages to Likert ATE $\approx 0$ (n.s.) while pairwise BT detects significant formal preference (63\%, $p < 0.001$). Content equivalence verified by NLI (0.998).}
23	\label{fig:blindspot_example}
24	\end{figure}

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	
5	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
6	\label{sec:stat_framework}
7	
8	We designate a primary family of six hypothesis tests (three judges $\times$ two axes: formality and verbosity) and control the family-wise error rate with Holm-Bonferroni correction at $\alpha = 0.05$.
9	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports both raw and adjusted $p$-values.
10	Analyses outside this grid are classified as either \emph{exploratory} (NLI threshold sensitivity sweep, \S\ref{sec:nli_threshold}) or \emph{confirmatory} (consistency filter validation, \S\ref{sec:consistency}).
11	Exploratory analyses are reported with uncorrected $p$-values and interpreted as hypothesis-generating; confirmatory analyses test specific hypotheses about pipeline validity and are evaluated at the nominal $\alpha = 0.05$ level without additional correction, as they address orthogonal questions about measurement reliability rather than the primary style-bias family.
12	Bradley-Terry parameters are estimated via spectral initialization followed by minorization-maximization \citep{hunter2004mm}, using the default regularization of the \texttt{choix} Python library.
13	All bootstrap confidence intervals use 10{,}000 pair-level resamples with seed 42.
14	
15	\paragraph{Model selection.}
16	Target judges span four architectures: GPT-4o (2024-08-06), the dominant judge powering AlpacaEval and MT-Bench leaderboards; Qwen3-32B-Instruct, also serving as rewriter for methodological consistency; Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct, the latest dense Llama model (Llama~4's MoE architecture introduces an additional confound); and Gemini-2.5-Flash as a confirmatory fourth architecture. Claude Sonnet~4 serves as negative control.
17	We pin versions for reproducibility; CSD applies to any black-box judge.

140	\citet{jeong2025comparative} show that pairwise comparison amplifies style biases (the Comparative Trap).
141	While format-level amplification of micro-preferences cannot be fully excluded, six independent controls bound this risk: (i)~NLI gate ablation reverses NLI-fail pairs to BT 41.7\% (Appendix~\ref{app:nli_ablation}); (ii)~Claude Sonnet 4 shows no bias with the identical pairwise format (BT 56.2\%/54.1\%, n.s.); (iii)~cross-axis BT variation (register 55\% $<$ formality 67\% $<$ verbosity 77\%) is inconsistent with uniform format amplification; (iv)~rewriting artifact absorbed by GEE decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:artifact_control}); (v)~debiased prompting partially reduces bias (verbosity $-13.1$pp; formality $-5.7$/$-0.4$pp); (vi)~four independent architectures all confirm the Blindspot with consistent GEE confound structure (\S\ref{sec:cross_judge}).
142

144	\centering
145	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/power_curve.pdf}
146	\caption{Detection probability at observed effect magnitudes. 80\% power crossings: sign test $n \approx 62$, BT $n \approx 114$, binarized Likert $n \approx 462$, Likert ATE $n \approx 1{,}473$. At typical scales ($n = 50$--$200$, shaded), pairwise methods reach 80\% power while Likert methods remain far below. At $n = 355$, Likert $d = {-}0.025$ confirms the gap is structural, not merely a power issue.}
147	\label{fig:power_curve}
148	\end{figure}

148	\end{figure}
149	
150	\subsection{Cross-Judge Replication}
151	\label{sec:cross_judge}
152	
153	Three independent judges replicate the Blindspot: Qwen3-32B (BT 66.5\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.908$), Llama-3.3-70B (BT 69.5\%, $p = 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.846$), and Gemini-2.5-Flash (confirmatory: BT 70.8\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $d = 0.05$, n.s.), spanning four architectures.
154	For formality, Gemini shows a primarily originality-driven preference (BT 57.4\%, $p = 0.031$; Style OR $= 1.64$, n.s.; Orig OR $= 2.51$, $p = 0.002$)---originality-dominated rather than conditionally style-driven as in the other three judges, confirming that confound structure is both axis- and judge-dependent.
155	
156	\subsection{Cross-Axis Replication: Verbosity}
157	\label{sec:cross_axis}
158	
159	Verbosity provides the strongest Blindspot: all four target judges show consistent preference (GPT-4o 76.0\%, Qwen3-32B 77.3\%, Llama-70B 73.5\%, Gemini 70.8\%; all $p \leq 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant for all four ($p = 0.403$, $0.179$, $0.752$, $0.370$).
160	Verbosity is style-dominated across all four judges: GEE confirms massive style effects (OR $8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$) with originality ORs below 1 ($0.38$--$0.49$; only Llama-70B significant at $p = .020$), indicating artifacts favor rewrites rather than reflecting quality confounding (Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
161	Both rewriting directions favor verbose responses (90.7\%/80.6\%); controlling for length leaves the style OR unchanged ($<$2\%).
162	Evaluator-producer overlap applies only to Qwen3-32B (both rewriter and judge); an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds this to a $22\%$ Style OR reduction ($13.10 \to 10.17$; Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}). GPT-4o and Llama-3.3-70B use a separate rewriter and are not subject to self-preference bias.
163	
164	\subsection{Register: Judge-Dependent Confound Structure}
165	\label{sec:negative_control}
166	
167	Register shows judge-dependent heterogeneity: Llama-70B B

_source line 3992 · 2026-05-21T22:44:42.377198+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced Controlled Style Decomposition (CSD), a black-box framework yielding two findings across five judges, three benchmarks, and three style axes.
5	First, the Likert Blindspot: Likert scoring systematically misses style biases that pairwise comparison detects---complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$), ${>}60\%$ attenuation at stronger effects---confirmed by seven statistical methods.
6	Whether this reflects measurement attenuation or construct divergence, Likert-only audits miss style-driven preferences that propagate through alignment pipelines.
7	Second, GEE conditional decomposition demonstrates per-axis confound structure consistent across four target architectures---verbosity style-dominated, formality conditional, register judge-dependent---determining which estimates require conditional analysis and informing debiasing priorities.
8	
9	Pairwise comparison offers superior detection sensitivity for style bias, provided that amplification controls are in place (\S\ref{sec:amplification}); existing Likert-based null findings may reflect measurement insensitivity rather than genuine absence of bias.
10
